The moduli space of stable supercurves and its canonical line bundle
Giovanni Felder, David Kazhdan, Alexander Polishchuk

Abstract
. We prove that the moduli of stable supercurves with punctures is a smooth proper DM stack and study an analog of the Mumford’s isomorphism for its canonical line bundle.



1.
Introduction

The moduli space
{\mathcal{S}}_{g}
of supercurves of genus
g
(aka super Riemann surfaces, aka SUSY curves) has been around in mathematics and physics
since the 80’s. It plays an important role in superstring theory and has been studied using the language of algebraic geometry in
[
26
]
,
[
9
]
,
[
6
]
,
[
16
]
and
other works.
One long standing gap in the mathematical side of the story has been the study of the analog of Deligne-Mumford compactification by stable supercurves
(which is a proper Deligne-Mumford superstack).
One of the goals of the present paper is to contribute to filling this gap. This compactification seems to be necessary for continuing the study of superstring supermeasure (see

[
33
]
,
[
19
]
).

The definition of a stable supercurve and a sketch of a proof that this gives a smooth and proper DM-stack is contained Deligne’s letter
to Manin
[
13
]
(supernodes were also introduced independently in physics literature, see
[
10
]
). The main part of Deligne’s letter is
devoted to the infinitesimal part of the theory. In particular, he describes miniversal deformations of two types fo supernode singularities (see
Sec.
3.2
). The idea of the rest of the proof is to use the superanalog of the Artin’s criterion for proving algebraicity of a stack.

In the present paper we revisit Deligne’s letter and generalize its results to the case of stable supercurves with punctures.
One of the special features of the super-case is that instead of “marked points” we consider supercurves with
Neveu-Schwarz (NS) and Ramond punctures
.
Of these, NS punctures are like marked points: for a family of (stable) supercurves
X\to S
they are given by sections
S\to X
. However, Ramond punctures have a different
nature: they are given by relative Cartier divisors
R\subset X
such that the projection
R\to S
is smooth of dimension
0|1
, and also the definition of being a supercurve
is modified near
R
(see Sec.
2.2
for details). Note that the nodes of stable supercurves can also be of two types, NS and Ramond.

Theorem A
.
There exists a smooth and proper DM-stack
\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}_{g,n_{NS},n_{R}}
over
\C
representing the functor of families of stable supercurves of genus
g
with

n_{NS}
NS punctures and
n_{R}
Ramond punctures.

After we finished this work we learned about the work of Moosavian and Zhou
[
27
]
, where Theorem A is also proved.
Their work also contains a lot of useful foundational results in algebraic supergeometry, e.g., proves the existence of Hilbert superschemes.
Another work with some foundational results, including the existence of Hilbert and Picard superschemes, is the forthcoming paper
[
7
]
.

We mostly follow
[
13
]
in the part concerning deformation theory (extending it to the case of stable supercurves with punctures).
In the rest of the proof we avoid using the Artin’s criterion and give a more direct proof based on the existing solution of the corresponding purely even moduli problem
due to Cornalba and Jarvis. Namely, the functor of families of stable supercurves
restricted to even bases is precisely the functor of generalized spin structures considered in
[
11
]
,
[
22
]
.

In the course of proof of Theorem A we find a natural relatively ample line bundle on any family of stable supercurves with punctures (see Theorem
4.3
).
Although this is not strictly necessary for Theorem A (where one could use an algebraic space to replace the Hilbert scheme), this can be viewed as a super-analog
of the well known fact that the relative log canonical bundle is relatively ample for a family of stable curves.
The important difference is that in the case of a family of stable supercurves
X\to S
the relative canonical bundle on the smooth part does not extend to a line bundle on the entire
family (the extension is not locally free at NS nodes). However, we prove that its square does extend to a line bundle which we still denote as
\omega_{X/S}^{2}
.
This line bundle plays an important technical role in the rest of the paper. In the hindsight, this is not too surprizing since the restriction of
\omega_{X/S}^{2}
to the reduced part
of moduli of supercurves recovers the relative dualizing sheaf of the corresponding usual family of stable curves.

One point which is not highlighted in
[
13
]
is that there is a canonical effective Cartier divisor
\Delta
supported on the boundary of the compactification
\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}_{g}

(in the presence of odd variables, which are nilpotent, such a structure is not unique).
Essentially this structure is already seen from the study of deformations of the nodes of supercurves which can be of two types, NS and Ramond.
We also define a decomposition
\Delta=\Delta_{NS}+\Delta_{R}
corresponding to these two types of nodes
and find two global expressions for the line bundle
{\mathcal{O}}(\Delta)
using Berezinians of certain natural morphisms of sheaves associated with the universal curve
(see Sec.
7
).

The second goal of this work is to study the canonical line bundle
K_{\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}_{g}}

over
\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}_{g}
, i.e., the Berezinian of the cotangent vector bundle.
The super analog of Mumford isomorphism for smooth supercurves,
expressing the canonical line bundle of
{\mathcal{S}}_{g}
in terms of natural Berezinian line bundles was considered in

[
32
]
,
[
29
]
(see also
[
15
]
where some work is done in the punctured case).
Namely, it states that

K_{{\mathcal{S}}_{g}}\simeq\operatorname{Ber}_{1}^{5},

where

\operatorname{Ber}_{1}:=\operatorname{Ber}(R\pi_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{X})\simeq\operatorname{Ber}(R\pi_{*}\omega_{X/\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}_{g}}),

where
\pi:X\to{\mathcal{S}}_{g}
is the
universal stable supercurve.

In the case of the moduli of stable supercurves (and in the presence of punctures)
we still have the line bundle
\operatorname{Ber}_{1}
defined as above, however
the expression for the canonical bundle has to be corrected.

Theorem B
.
Let
\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}=\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}_{g,n_{NS},n_{R}}
.
There exists a canonical isomorphism

K_{\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}}\rTo{SM_{\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}}}\operatorname{Ber}_{1}^{5}\otimes\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n_{NS}}\Psi_{i}(-2\Delta_{NS}-\Delta_{R}),

where
\Delta_{NS}
and
\Delta_{R}
are the components of the boundary divisor corresponding to nodes of NS and Ramond type,
and
\Psi_{i}
is the line bundle associated with the
i
th NS puncture
P_{i}
:

\Psi_{i}:=P_{i}^{*}\omega_{X/\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}}.

In the case when there are no punctures the isomorphism becomes

K_{\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}_{g}}\simeq\operatorname{Ber}_{1}^{5}(-2\Delta_{NS}-\Delta_{R}).

Finally, we study the restriction of the super-Mumford isomorphism
SM_{\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}_{g}}
to the NS boundary divisor, and show that it is related to the similar isomorphisms for lower genus. As in the classical case, each component of
\Delta_{NS}
is an image of a natural gluing map
B\to\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}_{g}
,
where
B
is some lower genus moduli space with more punctures or a product of two such moduli spaces (see Sec.
7.5
).

Theorem C
.
Let
B\to\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}_{g}
be the gluing map to one of the components of
\Delta_{NS}
from a lower genus (uncompactified) moduli space
B
.
There exists a natural isomorphism (see below) of the normal bundle

N_{B}:={\mathcal{O}}(\Delta)|_{B}\simeq\Psi_{1}^{-1}\otimes\Psi_{2}^{-1},

such that the following diagram is commutative up to a sign

{diagram}

The identification of
N_{B}
in Theorem C comes from the identification of
\Delta
as the vanishing
locus of a morphism induced on Berezinians by the morphism

R\pi_{*}(\Omega_{X/\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}_{g}})\to R\pi_{*}j_{*}(\Omega_{U/\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}_{g}}),

where
j:U\to X
is the embedding of the smooth locus (see Sec. (
7.2
)).

The components of the Ramond boundary divisor
\Delta_{R}
have a more complicated relation to lower genus moduli spaces (see Sec.
7.6
), and in this case we formulate a conjectural analog of Theorem C in Sec.
9.3
.

The paper is organized as follows.
In Section
2
we review the basics on stable supercurves starting from the definitions.
In particular, in
2.4
we discuss standard local coordinates of smooth supercurves (near an ordinary point and near a Ramond
puncture).
Then in
2.5
we discuss a well-known correspondence
between the NS-punctures and divisors.

In Section
3
we study the deformation functor of a stable supercurve
X_{0}
with punctures.
The main result is that this functor is smooth (more precisely,
it is smooth over the product of deformation functors of the singularities of
X_{0}
). We start with infinitesimal results, reproducing Deligne’s calculation
of infinitesimal deformations of two types of super node singularities (see Theorem
3.3
) and identifying the sheaves of infinitesimal automorphisms of stable supercurves with punctures.
Then we study local deformations: of affine neighborhoods of a smooth point,
of a Ramond puncture, and of a singular point (see
3.5
,
3.6
and Lemma
3.18
).
Finally we prove the smoothness result for global deformations, Proposition
3.19
.

In Section
4
we prove that an extension of the square of the relative canonical bundle
\omega_{U/S}^{2}
from the smooth locus
U\subset X

of a sufficiently nice family
X\to S
of stable supercurves is a line bundle, and that after some corrections at the punctures, it becomes relatively ample
(see Theorems
4.1
and
4.3
).
We then show that the inverse of this line bundle shows up in the computation of the sheaf of infinitesimal automorphisms (see Theorem
4.4
).

In Section
5
we prove Theorem A. Mostly we use the previous results on deformation theory and the known results on the moduli of
generalized spin curves.

In Section
6
we study the behavior of the Kodaira-Spencer map for a family of smooth supercurves degenerating to a stable supercurve.
We consider the classical case of (even) curves in
6.1
, then the case of an NS node in
6.2
and the case of a Ramond node
in
6.3
. The main observation is that the Kodaira-Spencer map has a natural extension over the entire base involving
the subsheaf of the tangent space to the base consisting of vector fields preserving the degeneration divisor.

In Section
7
we study the boundary divisor
\Delta
of the moduli of stable supercurves. We give a definition of the boundary divisor as a Cartier divisor
(which is not automatic since we work with nonreduced spaces). We compute the corresponding line bundle in terms of the complex
[\Omega_{X/S}\to j_{*}\Omega_{U/S}]
, where
U\subset X
is the smooth locus of a family
X\to S
(see
7.2
). We prove
in
7.3
that
\Delta
is a normal crossing divisor and define in
7.4

the subdivisors
\Delta_{NS}
and
\Delta_{R}
corresponding to the NS and Ramond type nodes such that
\Delta=\Delta_{NS}+\Delta_{R}
.
Then we discuss in
7.5
and

7.6
the gluing maps from lower genus moduli spaces to the boundary components.
Note that in the case of a Ramond node there is an extra odd parameter involved in the gluing.

In Section
8
we study the canonical line bundle over the moduli of stable supercurves with the goal of proving Theorem B.
The identification of the canonical line bundle for the moduli of smooth supercurves is a combination of the isomorphism coming from the Kodaira-Spencer map and of an analog of
the Mumford’s isomorphism between different Berezinian line bundles. We have to investigate what happens with both these ingredients near the boundary divisor.
For the Kodaira-Spencer map this was done in Section
6
.
The main new nontrivial computation is that of the behavior of the super Mumford’s isomorphism as the supercurve degenerates (see
8.3
).
Extending this picture to supercurves with punctures is relatively easy and is done in
8.4
.

In Section
9
we prove Theorem C. Again the bulk of the argument is the study of the restriction of super Mumford’s isomorphism to the boundary divisor
in
9.1
. Then in
9.2
we do the same for the isomorphism coming from the Kodaira-Spencer map. In
9.3

we describe a conjectural picture for the case of a Ramond boundary component.

In Appendix
A
we prove a relative ampleness criterion for flat morphisms of superschemes (see Proposition
A.2
), which we
use to prove Theorem
4.3
.

Conventions

All the rings are assumed to be
\Z_{2}
-graded supercommutative with
1
.
We say that a ring (resp., a superscheme) is
even
if its odd component (resp., the odd component of the structure sheaf) is zero.
We often say “subscheme” for brevity where we should say “sub-superscheme”.
For a sheaf
F
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-modules on a superscheme
X
we denote by
F^{+}
and
F^{-}
its even and odd parts.
By a “bundle” on a superscheme we mean a (
\Z_{2}
-graded) locally free
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-module of finite rank. By a subbundle
F\subset E
in a bundle

E
we mean a (
\Z_{2}
-graded) locally free
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-submodule, which is locally a direct summand.
On any superscheme
X
we denote by
{\mathcal{N}}_{X}\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
the ideal generated by odd functions.
We denote by
X_{{\operatorname{bos}}}
the usual scheme with the same underlying topological space as
X
and with the structure sheaf

{\mathcal{O}}_{X}/{\mathcal{N}}_{X}
. We work over
\C
.

2.
Stable supercurves

In this section we discuss some basic facts about stable supercurves and their families, starting with definitions. In particular,
we discuss local descriptions of nodes and punctures, the correspondence between the NS punctures and divisors, and the
connection with generalized spin structures.

2.1.
Superstacks

We refer to
[
8
]
and
[
6
, Sec. 2]
for some basics on superschemes.
The notion of an algebraic (super)stack is developed similarly to the classical case (see
[
4
, Def. (5.1)]
):
one considers a category fibered in groupoids
{\mathcal{X}}
over the category of Noetherian superalgebras over
\C
,
which is a limit preserving stack (see
[
4
, (1.1)]
), such that the diagonal
{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{X}}\times{\mathcal{X}}
is
representable and there exists a smooth surjective morphism from a scheme to
{\mathcal{X}}
(see
[
9
, Sec. 3]
).
If the latter morphism can be chosen to be étale, then one gets the notion of Deligne-Mumford (DM) stack.

For example, it is proved in
[
9
]
that the moduli stack of (smooth) supercurves is a smooth separated DM stack over
\C
.

2.2.
Definition of families of supercurves

Definition 2.1
.

Let
S
be a superscheme. A family of
smooth supercurves with Ramond punctures
is a smooth
superscheme
X
over
S
of relative dimension
1|1
, equipped with a subbundle
{\mathcal{D}}\subset{\mathcal{T}}_{X/S}
of rank
0|1
,
such that the map given by the commutator of vector fields

{\mathcal{D}}^{\otimes 2}\to{\mathcal{T}}_{X/S}/{\mathcal{D}}

(2.1)

(which is a map of line bundles of rank
1|0
) is injective and its divisor of vanishing is the disjoint union

R=\sqcup_{i=1}^{n_{R}}R_{i},

where each
R_{i}
is smooth of dimension
0|1
over
S
. The components
R_{i}
are called
Ramond punctures
.

A distribution
{\mathcal{D}}
as above is often referred to as a
superconformal structure
on
X
.
An isomorphism of superschemes
X\simeq X^{\prime}
as above is called
superconformal
if it preserves the superconformal structures,
i.e., sends the distribution on
X
to the distribution on
X^{\prime}
.

In the above situation the map
D_{1}\otimes D_{2}\mapsto\frac{1}{2}[D_{1},D_{2}]
induces an isomorphism

{\mathcal{D}}^{\otimes 2}\rTo{\sim}{\mathcal{T}}_{X/S}/{\mathcal{D}}(-R).

(2.2)

Thus, we have an exact sequence

0\to{\mathcal{D}}\to{\mathcal{T}}_{X/S}\to{\mathcal{D}}^{\otimes 2}(R)\to 0,

or dually,

0\to{\mathcal{D}}^{-2}(-R)\to\Omega_{X/S}\to{\mathcal{D}}^{-1}\to 0.

From this we get an isomorphism
\omega_{X/S}:=\operatorname{Ber}(\Omega_{X/S})\simeq{\mathcal{D}}^{-1}(-R)
, or equivalently,

{\mathcal{D}}\simeq\omega_{X/S}^{-1}(-R).

(2.3)

Hence, taking the dual of the embedding
{\mathcal{D}}\to{\mathcal{T}}_{X/S}
, we get a surjective morphism

\delta:\Omega_{X/S}\to{\mathcal{D}}^{-1}\simeq\omega_{X/S}(R),

whose kernel is exactly the orthogonal to
{\mathcal{D}}\subset{\mathcal{T}}_{X/S}
.
Equivalently, we can view
\delta
as a derivation
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\to\omega_{X/S}(R)
, trivial on
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
.
Note that
{\mathcal{D}}
is recovered from
\delta
as the orthogonal to
\ker(\delta)
.

Example 2.2
.

Suppose
X
is an open subscheme in
{\mathbb{A}}^{1|1}_{S}
with relative coordinates
(z,\theta)
and

{\mathcal{D}}
is generated by
D=\partial_{\theta}+f\theta\partial_{z}
, for some even function
f
on
X
.
Then

\frac{1}{2}[D,D]=f\partial_{z},

so the condition that
{\mathcal{D}}
defines a structure of a supercurve on
X
(with no punctures)
is that
f
is invertible.
Thus, the canonical isomorphism

{\mathcal{D}}^{\otimes 2}\to{\mathcal{T}}_{X/S}/{\mathcal{D}}

sends
D\otimes D
to
f\partial_{z}\mod{\mathcal{D}}
.
Hence, the isomorphism

{\mathcal{D}}\rTo{\sim}\operatorname{Ber}({\mathcal{T}}_{X/S})\simeq\omega_{X/S}^{-1}

sends
D
to
f[dz|d\theta]^{-1}
, where
[dz|d\theta]
is a generator of
\omega_{X/S}
associated with the basis
(dz,d\theta)
of
\Omega_{X/S}
.
Thus, we can compute the dual of the embedding
{\mathcal{D}}\to{\mathcal{T}}_{X/S},

\delta:\Omega_{X/S}\to{\mathcal{D}}^{-1}\simeq\omega_{X/S}.

Namely,
\delta
sends
dz
to
\theta[dz|d\theta]
and
d\theta
to
f^{-1}[dz|d\theta]
. Thus, if we view
\delta
as a derivation,
we have

\delta(\phi)=D(\phi)\cdot f^{-1}[dz|d\theta].

Remark 2.3
.

Assume that
S
is even. Then for a smooth supercurve
(X,{\mathcal{D}})
over
S
, the bosonic truncation
C=X_{{\operatorname{bos}}}=(|X|,{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{+})
is a family of smooth curves over
S
,
and we have a natural projection
\pi:X\to C
. Furthermore, we can view
L={\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{-}
as a line bundle on
C
. Since
\pi
is a finite morphism, we have a natural identification of
\Z_{2}
-graded coherent sheaves on
C
,

\omega_{X}=\pi^{!}\omega_{C}=\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}({\mathcal{O}}_{X},\omega_{C})=\omega_{C}\oplus\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}(L,\omega_{C}),

so the odd part of the derivation
\delta
induces an isomorphism
L\rTo{\sim}\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}(L,\omega_{C})
, or equivalently
L^{2}\rTo{\sim}\omega_{C}
.
It is well known that under the above identification of
\omega_{X}^{+}
with
\omega_{C}
the even part of
\delta
is given by the de Rham differential
d:{\mathcal{O}}_{C}\to\omega_{C}

(e.g., this can be deduced from the local standard structure of smooth supercurves, see
[
13
]
or Sec.
2.4
below).

Definition 2.4
.

(i) A
supercurve with punctures
over
S
, is a superscheme
X
of finite type over
S
, flat and relatively Cohen-Macaulay, together with

•

a collection of disjoint closed subschemes called
NS-punctures
and
R-punctures

P_{i}\subset X,\ i=1,\ldots,n_{NS};\ \ R_{i}\subset X,i=1,\ldots,n_{R},

such that the projection
P_{i}\to S
is an isomorphism, and each
R_{i}
is a Cartier divisor (flat over
S
);

•

a derivation

\delta:{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\to\omega_{X/S}(R),

trivial on pull-backs of functions on
S
, where
R=\sum R_{i}
. Here
\omega_{X/S}
is the relative dualizing sheaf
so that
\pi^{!}{\mathcal{O}}_{S}=\omega_{X/S}[1]
(the fact that it is a sheaf follows from the Cohen-Macaulay property).

We impose the following additional properties.

•

There is an open fiberwise dense subset
U\subset X
such that
U/S
is smooth of dimension
1|1
,
and
P_{i}\subset U
,
R_{i}\subset U
;

•

the derivation
\delta
corresponds to a structure of a smooth supercurve with Ramond punctures on
(U,(R_{i}))
over
S
;

•

on every geometric fiber
X_{s}
,
\delta^{-}
induces an isomorphism

{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{s}}^{-}\rTo{\sim}\omega_{X_{s}}(\sum R_{is})^{-};

(2.4)

By an
isomorphism
of supercurves with punctures over
S
we mean an isomorphism of superschemes over
S
compatible with all the structures.
We say that a relative vector field on
X
(i.e., a derivation of
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
, trivial on
\pi^{-1}{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
) is
superconformal
if it preserves
\delta
.

(ii) A supercurve with punctures
(X/S,P_{\bullet},R_{\bullet})
is called
stable (resp., prestable)
if
X
is proper over
S
and
for every geometric fiber
X_{s}
, passing to the bosonic truncation
(X_{s})_{{\operatorname{bos}}}
with the marked points induced by
(P_{i})
and
(R_{i})

one gets a usual stable (resp., prestable) pointed curve.

The role of the isomorphism (
2.4
) can be understood by looking at the case of even
S
(see Lemma
2.6
below).
As observed in
[
13
]
, if
U\subset X
is the maximal open subset which is smooth over
S
, then

\delta
induces a structure of a smooth supercurve on
U\setminus\cup_{i}R_{i}
over
S
.

Since Cohen-Macaulay property appears in the definition of supercurves, we recall some results involving it that will be useful for us.

Lemma 2.5
.

(i) Let
X
be a Cohen-Macaulay locally Noetherian superscheme,
j:U\to X
an open embedding such that
Z=X\setminus U
has codimension
\geq 2
(resp.,
\geq 1
) in
X
.
Then the natural map
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\to j_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{U}
is an isomorphism (resp., injective). Similarly, if
{\mathcal{F}}
is a Cohen-Macaulay coherent sheaf on
X
with full support, then the natural map

{\mathcal{F}}\to j_{*}j^{*}{\mathcal{F}}
is an isomorphism (resp., injective).

(ii) Let
X\to S
be flat, relatively Cohen-Macaulay, with fibers of pure dimension
1
, and let
j:U\hookrightarrow X
be a fiberwise dense open subscheme. Then the canonical map

\omega_{X/S}\to j_{*}j^{*}\omega_{X/S}

is injective.

(iii) Let
X
be a superscheme over a field
k
. Then
X
is Cohen-Macaulay with a dense open which is smooth of dimension
1|1
if and only
X_{{\operatorname{bos}}}
is a reduced curve,

{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{-}
is a Cohen-Macaulay sheaf with full support on
X_{{\operatorname{bos}}}
, locally free of rank
1
on a dense open, and
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{-}\cdot{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{-}=0
.

Proof.

(i) It is enough to check the vanishing of the corresponding local cohomology
\underline{H}^{i}_{Z}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})=\underline{H}^{i}_{Z}({\mathcal{F}})
for
i=0,1
(resp., for
i=0
). But this follows from
[
20
, Exp. VII,Cor. 1.4]
.

(ii) Otherwise, in some neighborhood of a point in
X
the kernel of this morphism
would be a nonzero subsheaf of
\omega_{X/S}
with finite support over
S
. So we would get a nonzero morphism

{\mathcal{O}}_{Z}\to\omega_{X/S}=\pi^{!}{\mathcal{O}}_{S}[-1],

with
Z
finite over
S
. By adjunction of the pair
(\pi_{*},\pi^{!})
, it would correspond to a nonzero morphism
\pi_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{Z}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{S}[-1]

which is impossible.

(iii) This follows easily from the interpretation of the Cohen-Macaulay condition for a superring in terms of the even and odd components given in
[
27
, Lem. 7.5]
.
∎

Supercurves with punctures over even bases can be described in purely even terms as follows (cf.
[
13
, 1.5]
).

Lemma 2.6
.

Assume that
S
is even.
Then the data of a supercurve
(X,P_{\bullet},R_{\bullet})
over
S
is equivalent to the following data:

•

a flat family of pointed curves
(C,p_{\bullet},r_{\bullet})
over
S
with reduced geometric fibers, smooth near the marked points;

•

a coherent sheaf
L
on
C
, flat and relatively Cohen-Macaulay over
S
, locally free of rank
1
over a fiberwise
dense open in
C
;

•

an isomorphism

L\rTo{\sim}\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}(L,\omega_{C}(\sum r_{i})).

(2.5)

Under this correspondence one has
|C|=|X|
,

{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{+}={\mathcal{O}}_{C},\ {\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{-}=L,\ \omega_{X}^{+}=\omega_{C},\ \omega_{X}^{-}=\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}(L,\omega_{C})\simeq L(-\sum r_{i}),

where
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{-}\cdot{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{-}=0
,
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{-}\cdot\omega_{X}^{+}=0
, and the action map
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{-}\otimes\omega_{X}^{-}\to\omega_{X}^{+}
is identified with
the natural evaluation map.
The puncture
P_{i}
is the image of the composition
S\rTo{p_{i}}C\to X
; and
R_{i}
is the schematic preimage of
r_{i}
under the projection

X\to C
. The isomorphism (
2.5
) corresponds to
\delta^{-}
.

Proof.

Let us consider the decomposition
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{+}\oplus{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{-}
into the even and odd components.
Then the bosonic truncation
C=X_{{\operatorname{bos}}}=(|X|,{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{+})
is a family of curves over
S
. Every geometric fiber
C_{s}
is a Cohen-Macaulay
curve, with a dense smooth open. Equivalently,
C_{s}
is a reduced curve.
Furthermore,
L={\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{-}
is a coherent sheaf on
C
with the claimed properties, by Lemma
2.5
(iii). Conversely, using the same lemma,
starting with
(C,L)
we get a superscheme over
S
with required properties by setting
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}={\mathcal{O}}_{C}\oplus L
(where the product
L\cdot L
is zero in
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
).

The marked points
P_{i}:S\to X
factor through marked points
p_{i}:S\to C
. On the other hand,
the Cartier divisors
R_{i}\hookrightarrow X
induce Cartier divisors
r_{i}\hookrightarrow C
. By assumption, the latter are also marked points on
C
.
We claim that
R_{i}
i exactly the preimage of
r_{i}
under the canonical projection
\pi:X\to C
. Indeed, this immediately follows from the fact that
locally
R_{i}
is given by an even equation
f\in{\mathcal{O}}_{X}^{+}={\mathcal{O}}_{C}
, and
r_{i}
is given by the same equation
f
in
C
.

We have a natural identification of
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-modules, compatible with the
\Z_{2}
-grading

\omega_{X}=\pi^{!}\omega_{C}=\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}({\mathcal{O}}_{X},\omega_{C}).

Taking the odd parts, we get an isomorphism

\omega^{-}_{X}\simeq\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}(L,\omega_{C}).

Thus,
\delta^{-}
induces an isomorphism (
2.5
). On the other hand,
\delta^{+}:{\mathcal{O}}_{C}\to\omega_{C}(\sum r_{i})
is induced by the usual de Rham differential
(since this is so on a dense open subset, see Remark (
2.3
)). Hence, we can recover
\delta
from the isomorphism (
2.5
).
∎

A coherent sheaf
L
on a prestable curve
C/S
equipped with an isomorphism (
2.5
) is called a
generalized spin structure
.

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
(X,P_{\bullet},R_{\bullet})
and
(X^{\prime},P^{\prime}_{\bullet},R^{\prime}_{\bullet}
) be a pair of supercurves over
S
,
and let
\varphi:X\rTo{\sim}X^{\prime}
be an isomorphism of superschemes over
S
, sending
P_{i}
to
P^{\prime}_{i}
and

R_{j}
to
R^{\prime}_{j}
. Assume that the restriction of
\varphi
to a fiberwise dense open subscheme
U\subset X
, which is smooth over
S
, is an isomorphism of smooth
supercurves. Then
\varphi
is an isomorphism of supercurves.

Proof.

Let
j:U\to X
be the inclusion.
By assumption, we have
\varphi^{*}\omega_{X^{\prime}/S}(R^{\prime})\simeq\omega_{X/S}(R)
.
Thus, we can view
\varphi^{*}\delta_{X^{\prime}}
as a derivation
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\to\omega_{X/S}(R)
, and we have
to prove the equality
\varphi^{*}\delta_{X^{\prime}}=\delta_{X}
. Since we know that this equality holds over
U
,
the assertion follows from Lemma
2.5
(ii).
∎

We will later need the following result about infinitesimal deformations of supercurves.

Lemma 2.8
.

(
[
13
, Prop. 1.7]
)
Let
X_{0}
be a supercurve with punctures over
\C
. Suppose an infinitesimal deformation
X
of
X_{0}
as a supercurve
over
\C[t]/(t^{2})
or over
\C[\tau]
, where
t
is even and
\tau
is odd, is trivial as a deformation of a superscheme. Then it is trivial as a deformation
of a supercurve.

Proof.

Using the correspondence of Lemma
2.6
, we get the data
(C,L,\varphi_{0})
, where
\varphi_{0}
is an isomorphism

\varphi_{0}:L\rTo{\sim}\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}(L,\omega_{C}),

so that
{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{0}}={\mathcal{O}}_{C}\oplus L
. It is convenient to think of
\varphi_{0}
as a map

\varphi_{0}:L^{\otimes 2}\to\omega_{C}.

The case of even base
\C[t]/(t^{2})
.
In this case we can still use Lemma
2.6
for
X
. Since the underlying superscheme is not deformed, this means that only the map
\varphi_{0}

is deformed to
\varphi=\varphi_{0}+t\psi
for some map
\psi:L\to\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}(L,\omega_{C})
. Since
\varphi_{0}
is an isomorphism, we can write
\psi=\varphi_{0}\circ a
,
where
a
is an endomorphism of
L
. Let us set

\alpha=\operatorname{id}+\frac{t}{2}a:L\rTo{\sim}L.

Then on the locus where
L
is locally free we can think of
a
as a function on
C
, so

\alpha^{\otimes 2}=\operatorname{id}+ta
, and

\varphi_{0}\circ\alpha^{\otimes 2}=\varphi_{0}+t\varphi_{0}\circ a=\varphi,

which implies that this equality holds everywhere, so the data

(C,L,\varphi_{0})
and
(C,L,\varphi)
are isomorphic.

The case of odd base
\C[\tau]
.
We have to consider possible supercurve structures
\delta:{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\to\omega_{X/S}
on
X=X_{0}\times S
over
S=\operatorname{Spec}(\C[\tau])
reducing to
\delta_{0}
on
X_{0}
over
\C
.
Extending
\delta_{0}
to
X
by extension of scalars, we can write

\delta=\delta_{0}+\tau D,

for some odd derivation
D:{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{0}}\to\omega_{X_{0}}
. Let

D_{0}:{\mathcal{O}}_{C}\to L,\ \ D_{1}:L\to\omega_{C}

be the
components of
D
. A simple local calculation shows that the condition for
\delta
to define a supercurve structure on the smooth part of
X
implies that
D_{1}
is determined by
D_{0}
.

We want to find an automorphism
\alpha
of
X/S
, trivial on
X_{0}
, such that
\delta
is obtained from
\delta_{0}
by conjugation by
\alpha
. Note that
\alpha
necessarily has form

\alpha(f)=f+\tau E(f),

where
E:{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{0}}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{0}}
is an odd derivation. We take
E
with the components

E_{0}=D_{0}:{\mathcal{O}}_{C}\to L,\ \ E_{1}=0:L\to{\mathcal{O}}_{C}.

An easy calculation (on a smooth locus) shows that
\alpha_{\omega}\delta_{0}\alpha^{-1}=\delta_{0}+\tau\widetilde{D}
produces
\widetilde{D}
with
\widetilde{D}_{0}=D_{0}
. Here
\alpha_{\omega}:\omega_{X/S}\to\omega_{X/S}

is the map induced by
\alpha
. Hence
\widetilde{D}=D
, as required.
∎

2.3.
Dualizing sheaf on the formal completion

We will sometimes want to argue “formally locally”, so it will be useful to look at the obtained structure on the formal completion of
{\mathcal{O}}_{X,x}
for a supercurve
X/S
.
For this we use the duality theory for formal schemes developed in
[
2
]
(or rather, its superanalog). Namely, by the results of
[
2
, Sec. 2]
(extended to the supercase),
we can identify the completion

\hat{\omega}_{X,x}
with the dualizing sheaf for the morphism of formal superschemes
\hat{X}=\operatorname{Spf}(\hat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{X,x})\to\hat{S}=\operatorname{Spf}(\hat{{\mathcal{O}}}_{S,s})
, where
s\in S
is the image of
x\in X
.
The structure derivation
\delta:{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\to\omega_{X/S}
induces a continuous derivation

\hat{\delta}:{\mathcal{O}}_{\hat{X}}\to\omega_{\hat{X}/\hat{S}},

which is compatible with the derivation
\hat{\delta}_{X_{s}}:{\mathcal{O}}_{\hat{X}_{s},x}\to\omega_{\hat{X}_{s}/k(s)}
, where
\hat{X}_{s}
is the formal neighborhood of
x
in
X_{s}
.
In particular, this will allow us to make sense of deformations of supercurve singularities in Sec.
3.2
.

The following formal local description of
\omega_{X/S}
will be useful (this is a particular case of
[
13
, 1.6]
).

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
S=\operatorname{Spec}(R)
, where
R
is a local Artinian
\C
-superalgebra;
C=\operatorname{Spf}(B)
, with
B=R[\![w_{1},w_{2}]\!]/(w_{1}w_{2})
;

X=\operatorname{Spf}(A)
, where
B\subset A
is a finite extension of superalgebras. Assume that for
i=1,2
, there exists an odd element
\theta_{i}\in A[w_{i}^{-1}]
, such that

A[w_{i}^{-1}]\simeq R(\!(z_{i})\!)[\theta_{i}],

where
w_{i}=z_{i}^{d}
for some
d>0
.
Then for every
\eta_{i}\in\omega_{X/S}[w_{i}^{-1}]
, for
i=1,2
,
we can write

\eta_{i}=(a_{i}(z_{i})+b_{i}(z_{i})\theta_{i})[dz_{i}|d\theta_{i}],

where
a_{i}(z_{i})
and
b_{i}(z_{i})
are Laurent series.
We set
\operatorname{Res}_{z_{i}=0}(\eta_{i}):=\operatorname{Res}_{z_{i}=0}b_{i}(z_{i})dz_{i}
. Then

\omega_{X/S}:=\{(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\in\omega_{X/S}[w_{1}^{-1}]\oplus\omega_{X/S}[w_{2}^{-1}]\ |\ \eta_{1},\eta_{2}\text{ compatible and }\forall a\in A,\operatorname{Res}_{z_{1}=0}(a\eta_{1})+\operatorname{Res}_{z_{2}=0}(a\eta_{2})=0\},

where compatibility of
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{2}
means that they define the same element of
\omega_{X/S}[(w_{1}w_{2})^{-1}]
.

Proof.

Since
\pi:X\to C
is a finite morphism, we have

\omega_{X/S}\simeq\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}(\pi_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{X},\omega_{C/S}).

(2.6)

On the other hand, we have a well known description of
\omega_{C/S}(C)

as compatible pairs
(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})
, with
\eta_{i}\in\omega_{C/S}[z_{i}]
such that

\operatorname{Res}_{z_{1}=0}(b\eta_{1})+\operatorname{Res}_{z_{2}=0}(b\eta_{2})=0,

(2.7)

for every
b\in B={\mathcal{O}}(C)
.
Note that the isomorphism (
2.6
) is induced by the trace map
\tau:\pi_{*}\omega_{X/S}\to\omega_{C/S}
, whose localizations on
\omega_{X/S}[w_{i}^{-1}]
are given by

\tau([a(z_{i})+b(z_{i})\theta_{i}][dz_{i}|d\theta_{i}])=b(z_{i})dz_{i}.

The identification of
\omega_{X/S}(X)
with
\operatorname{Hom}_{B}(A,\omega_{C/S}(C))
, sends
\eta\in\omega_{X/S}(X)

to the map
A\to\omega_{C/S}(C):a\mapsto\tau(a\eta)
.

Now we use the fact that
\tau(a\eta)
is determined by the localizations
\tau(a\eta_{i})
,
i=1,2,

where
\eta_{i}\in\omega_{X/S}[w_{i}^{-1}]
,
which should satisfy the sum of residue condition (
2.7
).
It follows that
\eta
is determined by
\eta_{i}
,
i=1,2
, which should satisfy the condition

\operatorname{Res}_{z_{1}=0}(b\tau(a\cdot\eta_{1}))+\operatorname{Res}_{z_{2}=0}(b\tau(a\cdot\eta_{2}))=0

for any
a\in A
,
b\in B
. Since
\tau
is
B
-linear we can absorb the multiplication by
b
, so we obtain the claimed characterization.
∎

2.4.
Local descriptions

2.4.1.
Smooth supercurves with punctures

It is well known (see e.g.,
[
26
, Lem. 1.2]
or
[
16
, Lem. 3.1]
in the absolute case)
that locally in classical topology near a point of a smooth supercurve, there exist relative coordinates
(z,\theta)
such that

{\mathcal{D}}
is generated by
D=\partial_{\theta}+\theta\partial_{z}
and
\delta
is given by
\delta(f)=D(f)\cdot[dz|d\theta]
.
We show that the same assertion holds with respect to the étale topology and also consider an analogous statement for the case of Ramond punctures
(cf.
[
6
, Prop. 3.6]
).

Lemma 2.10
.

(i) Let
X/S
be a smooth supercurve and let
(z,\theta)
be a pair of even and odd local functions such that
dz
and
d\theta
generate the relative cotangent bundle near a point
p\in X
.
Then there exists another pair
(w,\eta)
like this defined in an étale neighborhood of
p
, with
w\equiv z\mod{\mathcal{N}}_{X}
, such that
{\mathcal{D}}
is generated
by
\partial_{\eta}+\eta\partial_{w}
.

(ii) Now let
X/S
be a smooth supercurve with a Ramond puncture
R\subset X
, and let
(z,\theta)
be a pair of even and odd local functions such that
dz
and
d\theta
generate the relative cotangent bundle and such that the ideal of
R
is generated by
z
.
Then locally in étale topology there exist a change of coordinates to
(w,\eta)
, with
w\equiv z\mod{\mathcal{N}}_{X}
,
such that
{\mathcal{D}}
is generated by
\partial_{\eta}+\eta w\partial_{w}
.

Proof.

(i) Let
{\mathcal{D}}
be generated by an odd vector field
D
of the form
D=f\partial_{\theta}+g\partial_{z}
. One has

\frac{1}{2}[D,D]\equiv f\partial_{\theta}(g)\cdot\partial_{z}\mod{\mathcal{N}}_{X}{\mathcal{T}}_{X/S}.

Note also that modulo nilpotents
D
reduces to
f\partial_{\theta}
.
Hence, in order for
D
and
[D,D]
to generate
{\mathcal{T}}_{X/S}
both
f
and
\partial_{\theta}(g)
should be invertible.

Thus, we can assume that
{\mathcal{D}}
is generated by some vector field of the form
D=\partial_{\theta}+g\partial_{z}
, where
g=g_{1}(z)+g_{0}(z)\theta
,
with
g_{0}
even and
g_{1}
odd.
Furthermore,
g_{0}
is invertible.
Let us look for
w
and
\eta
in the form

\displaystyle\eta=a_{0}(z)\cdot\theta,

\displaystyle w=z+a_{1}(z)\theta,

where
a_{0}
is invertible even and
a_{1}
is odd.
Changing to the new coordinates we get

D=[a_{0}+ga^{\prime}_{0}\theta]\partial_{\eta}+[-a_{1}+g(1+a^{\prime}_{1}\theta)]\partial_{w}.

In order for
{\mathcal{D}}
to be generated by
\partial_{\eta}+\eta\partial_{w}
we need
the equation

-a_{1}+g(1+a^{\prime}_{1}\theta)=(a_{0}+ga^{\prime}_{0}\theta)\cdot a_{0}\theta

to be satisfied. This is equivalent to the system

\displaystyle-a_{1}+g_{1}=0,

\displaystyle g_{0}+g_{1}a^{\prime}_{1}=a_{0}^{2}.

Since
g_{0}
is invertible, in an étale neighborhood we can choose
a_{0}
such that
a_{0}^{2}=g_{0}+g_{1}g^{\prime}_{1}
.
This
a_{0}
together with
a_{1}=g_{1}
is a solution.

(ii) Let
D=f\partial_{\theta}+g\partial_{z}
be a generator of
{\mathcal{D}}
. From the calculation in the beginning of part (i), we get an isomorphism

({\mathcal{T}}_{X/S}/{\mathcal{D}}+[{\mathcal{D}},{\mathcal{D}}])\otimes{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{\operatorname{bos}}}\simeq{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{\operatorname{bos}}}/(f\partial_{\theta}(g))\cdot\partial_{z}\oplus{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{\operatorname{bos}}}/(f)\cdot\partial_{\theta}

where
{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{\operatorname{bos}}}={\mathcal{O}}_{X}/{\mathcal{N}}_{X}
.
Thus, this sheaf surjects onto
{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{\operatorname{bos}}}/(f)\oplus{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{\operatorname{bos}}}/(f)
.
Since this quotient has to be isomorphic to
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}/({\mathcal{N}}_{X}+(z))
, we deduce that
f
is invertible modulo
{\mathcal{N}}
, so
f
is invertible.

Thus, we can assume that
{\mathcal{D}}
is generated by
D=\partial_{\theta}+g\partial_{z}
, where

g=g_{1}(z)+g_{0}(z)\theta
. Then we have

\frac{1}{2}[D,D]=[g_{0}+g_{1}g^{\prime}_{1}+(g_{1}g^{\prime}_{0}-g_{0}g^{\prime}_{1})\theta]\cdot\partial_{z},

where
f^{\prime}
denotes the derivative with respect to
z
.
In order for the commutator map
{\mathcal{D}}^{\otimes 2}\to{\mathcal{T}}_{X/S}/{\mathcal{D}}
to vanish exactly on the divisor
(z)
we should have

g_{0}+g_{1}g^{\prime}_{1}+(g_{1}g^{\prime}_{0}-g_{0}g^{\prime}_{1})\theta=z(u_{0}(z)+u_{1}(z)\theta),

with
u_{0}
invertible. In other words,

\begin{array}[]{l}g_{0}+g_{1}g^{\prime}_{1}=zu_{0},\\
g_{1}g^{\prime}_{0}-g_{0}g^{\prime}_{1}=zu_{1}.\end{array}

(2.8)

We claim that this implies that
g_{1}
is divisible by
z
. Indeed, let us write

g_{0}\equiv p_{0}+q_{0}z\mod(z^{2}),

g_{1}\equiv p_{1}+q_{1}z\mod(z^{2}),

where
p_{i}
and
q_{i}
are functions on the base. Note that since
g_{1}
is odd, from
the first of the equations (
2.8
) we get that
q_{0}
is invertible. Also, looking at the constant terms of these equations we get

\displaystyle p_{0}+p_{1}q_{1}=0,

\displaystyle p_{1}q_{0}-p_{0}q_{1}=0.

This implies that

p_{1}(q_{0}+q_{1}^{2})=p_{1}q_{0}=0.

Since
q_{0}
is invertible, we deduce that
p_{1}=0
which proves our claim that
g_{1}
is divisible by
z
.

Now we are going to make the same change of coordinates as in (i) with an additional constraint that
a_{1}
is divisible by
z
.
Since
\eta w=a_{0}z\theta
, we have to solve the equation

-a_{1}+g(1+a^{\prime}_{1}\theta)=(a_{0}+ga^{\prime}_{0}\theta)\cdot a_{0}z\theta,

or equivalently, the system

\displaystyle-a_{1}+g_{1}=0,

\displaystyle g_{0}+g_{1}a^{\prime}_{1}=a_{0}^{2}z.

Thus, we get the solution by taking
a_{0}
to be the square root of
u_{0}

and
a_{1}=g_{1}
(which is divisible by
z
).
∎

Definition 2.11
.

We refer to
(w,\eta)
as in Lemma
2.10
as
standard coordinates
.

By Example
2.2
, if
(z,\theta)
are standard coordinates on a smooth supercurve (away from punctures)
then the canonical derivation is given by

\delta(f)=(\partial_{\theta}+\theta\partial_{z})(f)\cdot[dz|d\theta].

On the other hand, if
(z,\theta)
are standard coordinates near a Ramond puncture then

\delta(f)=(\partial_{\theta}+\theta z\partial_{z})(f)\cdot[\frac{dz}{z}|d\theta].

2.4.2.
Nodes

In the rest of this subsection we consider only the absolute case, i.e.,
S=\operatorname{Spec}(\C)
.
Let
X
be a stable supercurve over
\C
. We denote by
(C,L)
be the underlying generalized spin curve, and fix a node

q\in C
. Recall that by definition
X
is required to be Cohen-Macaulay (CM).
Using the classification of CM sheaves of rank
1
on the nodal singularity
(see e.g.
[
21
, Sec. 1]
), we obtain that there are two possibilities for
L
:

•

L
is locally free near
q
. Then we say that this is a
Ramond node
(or
R-node
).

•

L
is the push forward of a line bundle on the normalization of
C
near
q
. Then we say that this is a
NS node
.

Furthermore, we have the following local descriptions near the nodes (see
[
13
]
), where the description of the generators
\omega_{X/S}
follows from Lemma

2.9
.

•

Near a Ramond node
X
has coordinates
(z_{1},z_{2},\theta)
(where
\theta
is odd) subject to
z_{1}z_{2}=0
. The complement of the node is the union of two branches

U_{1}
and

U_{2}
, where
z_{i}
is invertible on
U_{i}
, and
\omega_{X}
is free with the basis
{\mathbf{b}}
given by

{\mathbf{b}}=\begin{cases}[\frac{dz_{1}}{z_{1}}|d\theta]&\text{ on }U_{1},\\
-[\frac{dz_{2}}{z_{2}}|d\theta]&\text{ on }U_{2}.\end{cases}

The derivation
\delta
is given by

\delta(f)=\begin{cases}(\partial_{\theta}+\theta z_{1}\partial_{z_{1}})(f)\cdot{\mathbf{b}}&\text{ on }U_{1},\\
(\partial_{\theta}-\theta z_{2}\partial_{z_{2}})(f)\cdot{\mathbf{b}}&\text{ on }U_{2}.\end{cases}

•

Near an NS node
X
has coordinates
z_{1},z_{2},\theta_{1},\theta_{2}
subject to the equations

z_{1}z_{2}=z_{1}\theta_{2}=z_{2}\theta_{1}=\theta_{1}\theta_{2}=0.

The complement of the node is given again as the union of two branches
U_{1}
and
U_{2}
, where
z_{i}
is invertible on
U_{i}

and
(z_{i},\theta_{i})
form coordinates on
U_{i}
. The generators
[dz_{1}|d\theta_{1}]
and
[dz_{2}|d\theta_{2}]
of
\omega_{U_{1}}
and of
\omega_{U_{2}}
extend to sections
s_{1}
and
s_{2}

of
\omega_{X}
(zero on another branch), however, they do not generate it: there is an extra section

s_{0}=\begin{cases}\frac{\theta_{1}}{z_{1}}[dz_{1}|d\theta_{1}]&\text{ on }U_{1},\\
-\frac{\theta_{2}}{z_{2}}[dz_{2}|d\theta_{2}]&\text{ on }U_{2}.\end{cases}.

The derivation
\delta
is given by

\delta(f)=\begin{cases}(\partial_{\theta_{1}}+\theta_{1}\partial_{z_{1}})(f)\cdot{\mathbf{b}}&\text{ on }U_{1},\\
(\partial_{\theta_{2}}+\theta_{2}\partial_{z_{2}})(f)\cdot{\mathbf{b}}&\text{ on }U_{2}.\end{cases}.

Remark 2.12
.

The notions of NS node and NS puncture (resp., Ramond node and Ramond puncture) are related via the gluing construction that will be discussed in Sec.
7
.
Namely, two prestable supercurves with NS punctures
(X_{1},P_{1})
,
(X_{2},P_{2})
can be glued along their NS punctures forming a new prestable supercurve with an NS node.
Similarly, one can glue two Ramond punctures into a Ramond node.

2.5.
Correspondence between NS-punctures and divisors

Here we recall a natural correspondence between NS-punctures
P\subset X
and effective Cartier divisors
D
supported at
P
, for a smooth supercurve
X/S

(with arbitrary base
S
), see
[
28
]
,
[
5
]
. Note this correspondence only uses a neighborhood of
P
in
X
, so it can also be applied for stable supercurves with punctures
of both types, i.e, for each NS-puncture
P_{i}
we have a natural Cartier divisor
D_{i}
supported at
P_{i}
.

It is based on the fundamental fact that for a smooth supercurve
X/S
, the composed map

{\mathcal{T}}^{sc}_{X/S}\to{\mathcal{T}}_{X/S}\to{\mathcal{T}}_{X/S}/{\mathcal{D}}\simeq{\mathcal{D}}^{\otimes 2}\simeq\omega_{X/S}^{-2}

(2.9)

is an isomorphism of sheaves, where
{\mathcal{T}}^{sc}_{X/S}\subset{\mathcal{T}}_{X/S}
is the subsheaf of superconformal vector fields (those preserving
{\mathcal{D}}
).
Indeed, the proof is easily obtained from the existence of standard coordinates as in Lemma
2.10

(see
[
26
, Lem. 2.1]
). We denote by

\alpha:\omega_{X/S}^{-2}\rTo{\sim}{\mathcal{T}}^{sc}_{X/S}

the inverse isomorphism.

Definition 2.13
.

We say that an effective relative Cartier divisor
D\subset X

corresponds
to an NS-puncture
P\subset X
if
D
is supported at
P

and the following property holds. If the ideal of
D
is generated
locally by a function
f
then the ideal of
P
is generated by
(f,A(f))
, where
A
is any generator of the distribution
giving the superconformal structure. Note that in this case we necessarily have an inclusion of subschemes
P\subset D
.

The existence and uniqueness of a divisor
D
corresponding to an NS-puncture
P
is easy to check using standard coordinates
(z,\theta)
.
Namely, with respect to such coordinates the ideal of
P
has form
(z,\theta-a)
, for some odd function
a
on the base.
Then corresponding divisor
D
is given by the ideal
(z+a\theta)
. In the next lemma we give a coordinate-free characterization of this correspondence.

Lemma 2.14
.

For a smooth supercurve
X/S
and an NS-puncture
P\subset X
there exists a unique effective relative Cartier divisor
D
,
such that the following square is commutative

\begin{diagram}

(2.10)

where
\beta
is an isomorphism of sheaves of
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
-modules.
Furthermore, the divisor
D
corresponds to the NS-puncture
P
in the sense of Definition
2.13
.

Proof.

Let
\alpha_{P}:\omega_{X/S}^{-2}\to{\mathcal{T}}_{X/S}|_{P}
denote the composition of
\alpha
with the projection to
{\mathcal{T}}_{X/S}|_{P}
.
The commutativity of the square (
2.10
) together with injectivity of
\beta
imply that the kernel of
\alpha_{P}
is exactly
the subsheaf
\omega_{X/S}^{-2}(-D)\subset\omega_{X/S}^{-2}
. This shows the uniqueness of
D
.

Now let us construct
\beta
and show the commutativity of (
2.10
) for
P
given by the ideal
(z,\theta-a)
and
D
given by the ideal

(z+a\theta)
, where
(z,\theta)
are standard coordinates and
a
is an odd function on the base.
Note that the isomorphism
\alpha
locally has form

f\cdot{\mathbf{b}}^{-2}\mapsto f\cdot\partial_{z}+\frac{1}{2}(-1)^{|f|}A(f)\cdot A,

where
A=\partial_{\theta}+\theta\partial_{z}

and
{\mathbf{b}}=[dz|d\theta]
is a generator of
\omega_{X}
(see the proof of
[
26
, Lem. 2.1]
, where however the factor
\frac{1}{2}
is missing).
It is easy to see that

\alpha_{P}((z+a\theta)f\cdot{\mathbf{b}}^{-2})=0,

while
\alpha_{P}({\mathbf{b}}^{-2})
and
\alpha_{P}(\theta\cdot{\mathbf{b}}^{-2})
form an
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
-basis of
{\mathcal{T}}_{X/S}|_{P}
.
Since
{\mathbf{b}}^{-2}
and
\theta\cdot{\mathbf{b}}^{-2}
project to an
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
-basis of
\omega_{X/S}^{-2}|_{D}
, this shows that the assertion holds with

\beta({\mathbf{b}}^{-2})=\alpha_{P}({\mathbf{b}}^{-2}),\ \ \beta(\theta\cdot{\mathbf{b}}^{-2})=\alpha_{P}(\theta\cdot{\mathbf{b}}^{-2}).

∎

Note that since the composition
P\hookrightarrow D\to S
is an isomorphism, the composition
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{D}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{P}={\mathcal{O}}_{D}/(I_{P}/I_{D})
is an isomorphism,
which means that we have a canonical splitting

{\mathcal{O}}_{D}\simeq{\mathcal{O}}_{S}\oplus I_{P}/I_{D}.

(2.11)

We will use this splitting later (see Remark
8.10
).

Corollary 2.15
.

The map (
2.9
)
induces an isomorphism of sheaves

{\mathcal{T}}^{sc}_{(X,P)/S}\rTo{\sim}({\mathcal{T}}_{X/S}/{\mathcal{D}})(-D)\simeq\omega_{X/S}^{-2}(-D),

where
{\mathcal{T}}^{sc}_{(X,P)/S}\subset{\mathcal{T}}^{sc}_{X/S}
is the sheaf of superconformal vector fields preserving the ideal of
P
.

Proof.

This follows from the fact that the map
\alpha
induces an isomorphism of the kernels of the vertical arrows in the commutative square (
2.10
).
The kernel of the left vertical arrow in this square is
\omega_{X/S}^{-2}(-D)
, while the kernel of the right vertical arrow is
{\mathcal{T}}^{sc}_{(X,P)/S}
.
∎

Remark 2.16
.

For a general smooth family
X\to S
of relative dimension
(1|1)
there is a canonical dual family
\hat{X}\to S
, parametrizing irreducible Cartier divisors in the fibers of
X\to S

(see e.g.,
[
5
]
). The double dual
\hat{\hat{X}}
is canonically identified with
X
. The superconformal structure on
X/S
can be viewed as an isomorphism of
X
with
\hat{X}
.

2.6.
Supercurves over even bases and spin curves

If we only consider even bases, the functor of (stable) supercurves with punctures coincides with the functor
of stable curves with punctures equipped with generalized spin structures
i.e., coherent sheaves
L
as in Lemma
2.6
.
Note that in the setting of Lemma
2.6
we still refer to the two kinds of marked points
p_{\bullet}
and
r_{\bullet}
and NS and Ramond punctures.

The moduli stack
\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}_{g,n_{NS},n_{R}}
of stable spin curves of genus
g

with
n_{NS}
NS-punctures and
n_{R}
Ramond punctures (where
n_{R}
is necessarily even)
was studied in
[
22
]
,
[
23
]
, as a particular case of the stack of stable
r
-spin curves.
In particular, it shown to be a smooth proper Deligne-Mumford stack.
Note that although for
r>2
there are different versions of the functor of stable
r
-spin curves, however, in the case
r=2
, they all coincide (see

[
21
]
).

Let us recall how the generalized spin structures over stable curves look like.
A generalized spin structure
L
over a smooth curve
C
with punctures is a line bundle
L
equipped with an isomorphism

L^{2}\simeq\omega_{C}(r_{1}+\ldots+r_{n}),

where
(r_{\bullet})
are the Ramond punctures.

Now let
(C,p_{\bullet},r_{\bullet})
be a stable curve, and
let
\rho:\widetilde{C}\to C
be the partial normalization map, resolving a single node
q\in C
.
Let us equip
\widetilde{C}
with punctures in the following way:
first, it inherits all the punctures (NS and Ramond) of
C
. Secondly,
we mark the two points in
\rho^{-1}(q)
as two NS (resp., Ramond) punctures on
\widetilde{C}
if
q
is a NS (resp., Ramond)
node on
C
.

For a generalized spin structure
L
over
C

let us define the sheaf
\widetilde{L}
on
\widetilde{C}
as the quotient of
\rho^{*}L
by the torsion subsheaf.
Then
\widetilde{L}
is a line bundle on
\widetilde{C}
and

\widetilde{L}
is a generalized spin structure on
\widetilde{C}
with NS and Ramond punctures defined as above.

More precisely, if
q
is a Ramond node then
L
is locally free near
q
, so
\rho^{*}L
is still locally free on
\widetilde{C}
and
\widetilde{L}=\rho^{*}L
.
If
q
is an NS node then
L
is locally isomorphic to the ideal of the node, so
\rho^{*}L
acquires a nontrivial torsion. A local computation shows that the quotient
\widetilde{L}
of
\rho^{*}L

by the torsion subsheaf is locally free, and the composition
of the natural maps
L\to\rho_{*}\rho^{*}L\to\rho_{*}\widetilde{L}
is an isomorphism.

Remark 2.17
.

The coarse moduli of the stack
\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}_{g,n_{NS},n_{R}}
considered in
[
22
]
,
[
23
]
gives the same compactification of the classical moduli space
of spin curves as the one constructed by Cornalba
[
11
]
.
On the other hand, there is a different moduli stack constructed in
[
1
]
(which gives the same coarse moduli as the other constructions),
where stable curves are replaced by certain stacky curves. We will not use the latter moduli stack in this paper.

3.
Deformations

In this section we will study the deformation theory of stable supercurves (with punctures). In particular, we will prove that the deformation functor of a stable supercurve is smooth
and will compute its tangent space.

3.1.
Deformation functors on Artin superalgebras

The development of deformation theory in the super context goes back to
[
31
]
, see also
[
27
, Sec. 7.1.9]
.
We consider the category
\operatorname{Art}_{\C}
of local Artinian
\C
-superalgebras with the residue field
\C
.
Recall that a surjection
A\to B
is called a
small extension
if
{\mathfrak{m}}\ker(A\to B)=0
,
where
{\mathfrak{m}}
is the maximal ideal in
A
. Every surjection in
\operatorname{Art}_{\C}
is a composition of a finite number of small extensions.

We apply some standard results of deformation theory (say, those in
[
18
]
), or rather their superanalogs (which are straightforward to prove).
Recall that for a set-valued functor
F
on
\operatorname{Art}_{\C}
such that
F(\C)=\{*\}
, and for morphisms
A^{\prime}\to A
,
A^{\prime\prime}\to A
in
\operatorname{Art}_{\C}
one has a natural map

F(A^{\prime}\times_{A}A^{\prime\prime})\to F(A^{\prime})\times_{F(A)}F(A^{\prime\prime})\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ (\star)

One considers the following Schlessinger conditions on
F
:

(H1) The map
(\star)
is surjective if
A^{\prime\prime}\to A
is a small extension;

(H2) The map
(\star)
is an isomorphism if
A=\C
and
A^{\prime\prime}\to A
is a small extension.

We refer to functors satisfying (H1) and (H2) as
deformation functors
. Such a functor
F
is called
smooth
if
F(A^{\prime})\to F(A)
is surjective for any small extension
A^{\prime}\to A
.
More generally, a morphism of deformation functors
F\to G
is called
smooth
if for every small extension
A^{\prime}\to A
in
\operatorname{Art}_{\C}
, the natural map

F(A^{\prime})\to G(A^{\prime})\times_{G(A)}F(A)

is surjective.

Note that the
tangent space

t_{F}
to a deformation functor
F
is defined as the sum of even and odd components given by

t_{F}^{+}=F(\C[\epsilon]/\epsilon^{2}),\ \ t_{F}^{-}=F(\C[\tau]/\tau^{2}),

where
\epsilon
is even and
\tau
is odd. Elements of
t_{F}^{+}
(resp.,
t_{F}^{-}
) are referred to as even (resp., odd)
infinitesimal
deformations.

A morphism
h_{R}\to F
from the functor
h_{R}
pro-representable by a Noetherian complete local
\C
-algebra
\C
to a deformation functor
F
, is called the
hull
of
F
if
it is smooth, and the induced map on tangent spaces is an isomorphism. In this case we also say that the corresponding deformation over
R
is
miniversal
.

3.2.
Miniversal deformations of the nodes

In this subsection, following Deligne
[
13
]
, we describe miniversal deformations of two types of nodal singularities of prestable supercurves.
First, we give a precise definition of what do we mean by deformations of supercurve singularities.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
A/\C
be the completion of the local ring of a singular point on a supercurve over
\C
, so it is equipped with a derivation
\delta_{A}:A\to\omega_{\operatorname{Spf}(A)/\C}

(see Sec.
2.3
). By a
deformation of
A
over a local Artinian superalgebra
R
we mean a flat
R
-superalgebra
B
equipped with an isomorphism

B\otimes_{R}\C\simeq A
, and a derivation
\delta_{B}:B\to\omega_{\operatorname{Spf}(B)/R}
, inducing
\delta_{A}
under the reduction with respect to the homomorphism
R\to\C
.

We begin by describing two families of supercurves over
S=\operatorname{Spec}(\C[t])
, where
t
is even.

3.2.1.
Ramond node

Define
X/S
as a subscheme of the affine space
S\times{\mathbb{A}}^{2|1}
over
S
with coordinates
z_{1},z_{2},\theta
, given
by the equation

z_{1}z_{2}=t.

Note that we have a natural trivialization
{\mathbf{b}}
of
\omega_{X/S}
.

Over the open subset where
z_{1}\neq 0
(resp.,
z_{2}\neq 0
), we have
{\mathbf{b}}=[\frac{dz_{1}}{z_{1}}|d\theta]

(resp.,
{\mathbf{b}}=[-\frac{dz_{2}}{z_{2}}|d\theta]
), and
\delta
is given by
(\partial_{\theta}+\theta z_{1}\partial_{z_{1}})\cdot{\mathbf{b}}

(resp.,
(\partial_{\theta}-\theta z_{2}\partial_{z_{2}})\cdot{\mathbf{b}}
).

3.2.2.
NS node

Define
X/S
as a subscheme of the affine space
S\times{\mathbb{A}}^{2|2}
over
S
with coordinates
z_{1},z_{2},\theta_{1},\theta_{2}
, given
by the equations

z_{1}z_{2}=-t^{2},\ \ z_{1}\theta_{2}=t\theta_{1},\ \ z_{2}\theta_{1}=-t\theta_{2},\ \ \theta_{1}\theta_{2}=0.

(3.1)

Over the chart where
z_{i}\neq 0
(
i=1,2
),

\delta(f)=(\partial_{\theta_{i}}+\theta_{i}\partial_{z_{i}})(f)[dz_{i}|d\theta_{i}].

(3.2)

Remark 3.2
.

We follow the choice of signs in
[
13
]
in the equations (
3.1
). Note that one can get rid of signs replacing
z_{1}
by
-z_{1}
and
\theta_{1}
and
-\theta_{1}
. As was pointed out by the anonymous referee, there is a natural way to see this deformation of the NS node as a quotient of the Ramond node deformation
z_{1}z_{2}=t
by the
\Z_{2}
-action

z_{1}\mapsto-z_{1},\ \ z_{2}\mapsto-z_{2},\ \ \theta\mapsto-\theta.

Namely, the algebra of functions on this
\Z_{2}
-quotient is generated by
Z_{1}=z_{1}^{2}
,
Z_{2}=z_{2}^{2}
,
\Theta_{1}=z_{1}\theta
and
\Theta_{2}=z_{2}\theta
subject to relations obtained from
(
3.1
) by changing all signs to
+
.

The following result is proved in
[
13
]
. For the convenience of the reader we provide the proof below, with some details elaborated.

Theorem 3.3
.

The families over
\C[\![t]\!]
induced by the above two families are miniversal deformations of the completed rings of the two types of nodes.

Proof.

Let us denote by
X_{0}
the formal spectrum of the completed algebra
R
of one of the two types of nodes over
\C
(see
2.4.2
).
We denote by
F
the functor on
\operatorname{Art}_{\C}
of deformations of
X_{0}
as a formal supercurve (see Definition
3.1
.
It is easy to see that this functor satisfies (H1) and (H2).
Namely, in the context of (H1), if
X/A
(resp.,
X^{\prime}/A^{\prime}
,
X^{\prime\prime}/A^{\prime\prime}
) is a deformation of
X_{0}
over
A
(resp.,
A^{\prime}
,
A^{\prime\prime}
), then
{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{X}}:={\mathcal{O}}_{X^{\prime}}\times_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}\times{\mathcal{O}}_{X^{\prime\prime}}

can be viewed as a superscheme deformation
\widetilde{X}
of
X_{0}
over
\widetilde{A}:=A^{\prime}\times_{A}A^{\prime\prime}
inducing
X^{\prime}/A^{\prime}
and
X^{\prime\prime}/A^{\prime\prime}
. Furthermore, the map

{\mathcal{O}}_{X^{\prime}}\times_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X}}\times{\mathcal{O}}_{X^{\prime\prime}}\to\omega_{X^{\prime}/A^{\prime}}\times_{\omega_{X/A}}\omega_{X^{\prime\prime}/A^{\prime\prime}}\simeq\omega_{\widetilde{X}/\widetilde{A}}

induced by the supercurve structures on
X^{\prime}/A^{\prime}
and
X^{\prime\prime}/A^{\prime\prime}
, gives a supercurve structure on
\widetilde{X}/\widetilde{A}
. This proves the surjectivity needed for (H1).
In the case
A=\C
, if
\widetilde{X}
is any supercurve over
A^{\prime}\times A^{\prime\prime}
, let
X^{\prime}/A^{\prime}
,
X^{\prime\prime}/A^{\prime\prime}
be the induced supercurves over
A^{\prime}
and
A^{\prime\prime}
. Then we have the induced map

\alpha:{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{X}}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{X^{\prime}}\times_{{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{0}}}{\mathcal{O}}_{X^{\prime\prime}}

compatible with the supercurve structures. Since both algebras are flat over
A^{\prime}\times A^{\prime\prime}
and the map of algebras over
\C
, induced by
\alpha
, is the identity map on
{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{0}}
,

\alpha
is an isomorphism. This proves (H2).

It is enough to prove in both cases triviality of odd infinitesimal deformations and the fact that
the space of even infinitesimal deformations is a
1
-dimensional space with generators coming from the above families over
\C[t]
.
Indeed, assume we know this.
We have a natural morphism
G\to F
from the functor
G
prorepresentable by
\C[\![t]\!]
corresponding to one of our two families,
and by assumption, the map of tangent spaces
t_{G}\to t_{F}
is an isomorphism. Since the functor
G
is smooth, this implies that the morphism
G\to F
is smooth (and so
G\to F

is a hull of
F
, i.e., our deformation over
\C[\![t]\!]
is miniversal).

Let
A\to B
be a small extension with the kernel
I
. Then as is well known,
there exists a natural transitive action of
t_{F}\otimes_{\C}I
on every nonempty fiber of the map
F(A)\to F(B)
(and similarly for
G
).
Given
x_{B}\in G(B)
mapping to
y_{B}\in F(B)
, together with a lift
y_{A}\in F(A)
of
y_{B}
, we need to find
x_{A}\in G(A)
mapping to both
x_{B}
and
y_{A}
.
For this, first, we can find some
x^{\prime}_{A}\in G(A)
lifting
x_{B}\in G(B)
. Let
y^{\prime}_{A}\in F(A)
be the image of
x^{\prime}_{A}
. Then
y^{\prime}_{A}
is in the same fiber of
F(A)\to F(B)
as
y_{A}
,
so it is obtained from
y_{A}
by the action of an element
z\in t_{G}\otimes_{\C}I\simeq t_{F}\otimes_{\C}I
. Now we define
x_{A}
to be the element in the fiber of
G(A)\to G(B)
over

x_{B}
, obtained from
x^{\prime}_{A}
by the action of
-z
.

Thus, we are reduced to calculating the infinitesimal deformations. The calculation below is from
[
13
, Sec. 3]
.
First, we calculate the space
T^{1}:=T^{1}_{R/\C}
of first-order deformations of our superalgebra
R
over
\C
as
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(L_{R/\C},R)
, where
L_{R/C}
is the cotangent complex.
The latter is computed using a presentation of
R=S/I
as the quotient of a super polynomial ring
S
, and a presentation
I=\operatorname{coker}(F_{1}\to F_{0})
, where
F_{0}
and
F_{1}
are free

S
-modules. So the generators of
F_{0}
correspond to generators
(f_{i})
of the ideal
I
, while generators of
F_{1}
correspond to syzygies between
f_{i}
. Then
T^{1}
is given by the cohomology in the middle term of the complex

\operatorname{Hom}_{S}(\Omega_{S/\C},R)\rTo{d_{0}}\operatorname{Hom}_{S}(F_{0},R)\rTo{d_{1}}\operatorname{Hom}_{S}(F_{1},R),

where the first arrow sends a derivation
D:S\to R
to the map
e_{i}\mapsto D(f_{i})
.

In the case of the Ramond node, we have a single (even) relation
z_{1}z_{2}=0
and no syzygies, so
T^{1}
is identified with the quotient of
R
by the partial derivatives of

z_{1}z_{2}
, so the space
T^{1}
has dimension
(1,1)
. The corresponding universal deformation of the first order is given by the relation

z_{1}z_{2}=t+\tau\theta

(3.3)

over
\C[t,\tau]/(t^{2},t\tau)
(where
t
is even and
\tau
is odd).

In the case of the NS node, we have four relations

f=z_{1}z_{2},\ \ \phi_{1}=z_{1}\theta_{2},\ \ \phi_{2}=z_{2}\theta_{1},\ \ g=\theta_{1}\theta_{2}

and
4
generating syzygies

\sigma_{1}=\theta_{1}f-z_{1}\phi_{2},\ \ \sigma_{2}=\theta_{2}f-z_{2}\phi_{1},\ \ s_{1}=z_{1}g-\theta_{1}\phi_{1},\ s_{2}=z_{2}g+\theta_{2}\phi_{2}.

Thus, with respect to the dual bases of
\operatorname{Hom}_{S}(F_{i},R)
and the basis
\partial_{z_{1}},\partial_{z_{2}},\partial_{\theta_{1}},\partial_{\theta_{2}}
of
\operatorname{Hom}_{S}(\Omega_{S/\C},R)
, the maps
d_{0}
and
d_{1}

are given by

d_{0}=\left(\begin{matrix}z_{2}&z_{1}&0&0\\
\theta_{2}&0&0&z_{1}\\
0&\theta_{1}&z_{2}&0\\
0&0&\theta_{2}&-\theta_{1}\end{matrix}\right),\ \ d_{1}=\left(\begin{matrix}\theta_{1}&0&-z_{1}&0\\
\theta_{2}&-z_{2}&0&0\\
0&-\theta_{1}&0&z_{1}\\
0&0&\theta_{2}&z_{2}\end{matrix}\right).

We need to calculate
\ker(d_{1})/\operatorname{im}(d_{0})
.

For
v=af^{*}+b\phi_{1}^{*}+c\phi_{2}^{*}+dg^{*}\in\ker(d_{1})
, where
a,b,c,d\in R
, one has
z_{1}d=\theta_{1}b
, and
z_{2}d=-\theta_{2}c
. This implies that
d=\theta_{1}d_{1}+\theta_{2}d_{2}
.
Hence, modifying
v
by
d_{0}(\partial_{\theta_{1}})
and
d_{0}(\partial_{\theta_{2}})
(the last two columns of
d_{0}
), we can make
d=0
.
Let
H\subset\operatorname{Hom}_{S}(F_{0},R)
denote the span of
f^{*}
,
\phi_{1}^{*}
and
\phi_{2}^{*}
. We proved that
\ker(d_{1})=\ker(d_{1})\cap H+\operatorname{im}(d_{0})
.
Hence,

\ker(d_{1})/\operatorname{im}(d_{0})=\ker(d_{1})\cap H/\operatorname{im}(d_{0})\cap H.

Note that
d_{0}(p\partial_{z_{1}}+r\partial_{z_{2}}+s\partial_{\theta_{1}}+t\partial_{\theta_{2}})\in H
if and only if
\theta_{2}s=\theta_{1}t
, which is equivalent to
s,t\in(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})
.
Since
d_{0}(\theta_{1}\partial_{\theta_{1}})=d_{0}(\theta_{2}\partial_{\theta_{2}})=0
, we obtain that

\operatorname{im}(d_{0})\cap H=d_{0}(R\partial_{z_{1}}+r\partial_{z_{2}}+\theta_{2}\partial_{\theta_{1}}+\theta_{1}\partial_{\theta_{2}}).

Next, for
af^{*}+b\phi_{1}^{*}+c\phi_{2}^{*}\in\ker(d_{1})
we have
z_{1}c\in(\theta_{1})
,
z_{2}b\in(\theta_{2})
, which implies
b,c\in(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})
. Thus, adding multiples of
d_{0}(\partial_{z_{1}})

and
d_{1}(\partial_{z_{2}})
, we can assume
b\in(\theta_{1})
,
c\in(\theta_{2})
. Then the condition to be in
\ker(d_{1})
reduces to
a\in(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})
. This identifies
T^{1}
with the
quotient of the space of
af^{*}+b\phi_{1}^{*}+c\phi_{2}^{*}
with
a\in(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})
,
b\in(\theta_{1})
,
c\in(\theta_{2})
, modulo the submodule generated
by
\theta_{2}d_{1}(\partial_{z_{1}})
,
\theta_{1}d_{1}(\partial_{z_{2}})
,
d_{1}(\partial_{\theta_{1}})
and
d_{1}(\partial_{\theta_{2}})
. Thus, the quotient is
4
-dimensional with the basis

\theta_{1}f^{*},\ \ \theta_{2}f^{*},\ \ \theta_{1}\phi_{1}^{*},\ \ \theta_{2}\phi_{2}^{*}.

In other words, the universal deformation of the first order is given by the relations

z_{1}z_{2}+\tau_{1}\theta_{1}+\tau_{2}\theta_{2}=0,\ \ z_{1}\theta_{2}+t_{1}\theta_{1}=0,\ \ z_{2}\theta_{1}+t_{2}\theta_{2}=0,\ \ \theta_{1}\theta_{2}=0

(3.4)

over
\C[t_{1},t_{2},\tau_{1},\tau_{2}]/(t_{1}^{2},t_{1}t_{2},t_{2}^{2})
, where
t_{1},t_{2}
are even and
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}
are odd.

By Lemma
2.8
, the tangent space
t_{F}
to our deformation problem is a subspace in
T^{1}
, and over the restriction of the above family to
t_{F}
,
we have a derivation
\delta:{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\to\omega_{X/S}

deforming the derivation
\delta_{0}
on the special fiber
X_{0}
.
From now on we denote by
X/S
the formal superscheme given by either (
3.3
) or (
3.4
) over the superscheme
S=\operatorname{Spec}R
, corresponding
to an (even or odd)
1
-dimensional subspace in
T^{1}
.

Let
U_{i}/S
denote the localization of one of the families (
3.3
) or (
3.4
) obtained by inverting
z_{i}
, for
i=1,2
.
Then we have
{\mathcal{O}}(U_{i})\simeq R[z_{i},\theta_{i}]
, for
i=1,2
, so
\omega_{U_{i}/S}
is a free
{\mathcal{O}}(U_{i})
-module with the basis
[dz_{i}|d\theta_{i}]
.
On the other hand, it is easy to see that in both cases we have
z_{1}^{2}z_{2}^{2}=0
, so the corresponding algebra is a finite extension of
R[\![w_{1},w_{2}]\!]/(w_{1}w_{2})
, where
w_{i}=z_{i}^{2}
.
Thus, by Lemma
2.9
, we can characterize the pairs
\omega_{1}\in\omega_{U_{1}/S}
,
\omega_{2}\in\omega_{U_{2}/S}
corresponding to the global sections of
\omega_{X/S}
by the
condition

\operatorname{Res}_{z_{1}=0}(f_{1}\omega_{1})+\operatorname{Res}_{z_{2}=0}(f_{2}\omega_{2})=0

(3.5)

for any global function
f\in{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
with restrictions
f_{1}=f|_{U_{1}}
,
f_{2}=f|_{U_{2}}
.

Our strategy will be
to extend
\delta_{0}
to a pair of compatible derivations
\delta_{0,i}:{\mathcal{O}}_{U_{i}}\to\omega_{U_{i}/S}
,
i=1,2
, and then to see whether there exists a pair of compatible derivations

(\delta_{1},\delta_{2})
, with
\delta_{i}
vanishing over
U_{i}\cap X_{0}
, such that
(\delta_{0,1}+\delta_{1},\delta_{0,2}+\delta_{2})
takes
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
to
\omega_{X/S}

(we know that such
(\delta_{1},\delta_{2})
exists if and and only if
S
corresponds to the linear (super)-subspace of
t_{F}\subset T^{1}
.)
We will construct the extension
\delta_{0,1}
on
U_{1}
using the original formulas for
X_{0}
, and then will find a compatible
\delta_{0,2}

using equations (
3.3
) and (
3.4
).

Case of Ramond node
.

In this case we just have to check that
t_{F}^{-}
is zero. In other words, we have to consider the family given by
z_{1}z_{2}=\tau\theta
.
Note that
\omega_{X/S}
is free is one generator
{\mathbf{b}}
, such that

{\mathbf{b}}|_{U_{1}}=[\frac{dz_{1}}{z_{1}}|d\theta],\ \ {\mathbf{b}}|_{U_{2}}=[-\frac{dz_{2}}{z_{2}}|d\theta].

The derivations
\delta_{0,i}:{\mathcal{O}}_{U_{i}}\to\omega_{U_{i}/S}
are given by
f\mapsto D_{0,i}(f)\cdot{\mathbf{b}}
, where

D_{0,1}|_{U_{1}}(f)=\partial_{\theta}+\theta z_{1}\partial_{z_{1}},\ \ D_{0,2}|_{U_{2}}(f)=\partial_{\theta}-\theta z_{2}\partial_{z_{2}}.

We need to check that there are no compatible derivations
D_{i}:{\mathcal{O}}_{U_{i}\cap X_{0}}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{U_{i}\cap X_{0}}

such that
(D_{0,1}+\tau D_{1},D_{0,2}+\tau D_{2})
sends
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
to
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
.
For this we observe that

z_{1}|_{U_{1}}=z_{1},\ \ z_{1}|_{U_{2}}=\frac{\tau}{z_{2}}\cdot\theta.

Hence,

D_{0,1}(z_{1})|_{U_{1}}=\theta z_{1},\ \ D_{0,2}(z_{1})|_{U_{2}}=\frac{\tau}{z_{2}}.

Note that
D_{2}(z_{1})
is zero on
U_{2}\cap X_{0}
(since
z_{1}
vanishes on
U_{2}\cap X_{0}
), hence
(D_{0,1}+\tau D_{1},D_{0,2}+\tau D_{2})(z_{1})
would be a global function on
X
, whose restriction to
U_{2}
is equal to
\frac{\tau}{z_{2}}
,
which is impossible.

Case of NS node, odd deformations
.

Again we want to check the vanishing of
t_{F}^{-}
. We know that
t_{F}^{-}
is a subspace of the 2 dimensional odd vector space
(T^{1})^{-}
corresponding to the odd part of
the family (
3.4
). Note that we have an action of
{\mathbb{G}}_{m}^{2}
on the NS node
X_{0}
, so that the weights of the generating functions are

wt(\theta_{1})=(1,0),\ \ wt(\theta_{2})=(0,1),\ \ wt(z_{1})=(2,0),\ \ wt(z_{2})=(0,2).

The subspace
t_{F}^{-}
is invariant under the induced
{\mathbb{G}}_{m}^{2}
-action on
(T^{1})^{-}
. Since the weights of
\tau_{1}
and
\tau_{2}
are

wt(\tau_{1})=(1,2),\ \ wt(\tau_{2})=(2,1),

we see that if
t_{F}^{-}
is nonzero, it coincides with one of the coordinate lines in
(T^{1})^{-}
.

Thus, it is enough to study the restriction of the family (
3.4
) to the
\tau_{1}
direction:

z_{1}z_{2}=\tau_{1}\theta_{1},\ \ z_{1}\theta_{2}=z_{2}\theta_{1}=\theta_{1}\theta_{2}=0.

Using the description of the global sections of
\omega_{X/S}
as pairs
(\omega_{1},\omega_{2})\in\omega_{U_{1}/S}\oplus\omega_{U_{2}/S}
satisfying the residue condition (
3.5
),
we can lift the generators of
\omega_{X_{0}}
to global sections of
\omega_{X}
.
Namely, the generator
s_{1}=([dz_{1}|d\theta_{1}],0)
can be lifted to

\widetilde{s}_{1}:=([dz_{1}|d\theta_{1}],-\tau_{1}\frac{\theta_{2}}{z_{2}^{2}}[dz_{2}|d\theta_{2}])

(one has to apply the residue condition to the global function
z_{2}
).
On the other hand, the generator
s_{2}=(0,[dz_{2}|d\theta_{2}])
on
X_{0}
lifts to the same pair on
X
that we denote
\widetilde{s}_{2}
, and the generator

s=(\frac{\theta_{1}}{z_{1}}[dz_{1}|d\theta_{1}],-\frac{\theta_{2}}{z_{2}}[dz_{2}|d\theta_{2}])

on
X_{0}
lifts to the same pair on
X
that we denote
\widetilde{s}
.

The derivations
\delta_{0,i}:{\mathcal{O}}_{U_{i}}\to\omega_{U_{i}/S}
, for
i=1,2
, are still given by the formula (
3.2
). We need to show that for any choice of compatible
derivations

\delta_{i}:{\mathcal{O}}_{U_{i}}\to\omega_{U_{i}/S}
,
i=1,2
, the pair
(\delta_{0,1}+\tau_{1}\delta_{1},\delta_{0,2}+\tau_{1}\delta_{2})
does not take
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
to
\omega_{X/S}
.
Note that

(\delta_{0,1},\delta_{0,2})(\theta_{1},0)=([dz_{1}|d\theta_{1}],0)=\widetilde{s}_{1}+\tau_{1}(0,\frac{\theta_{2}}{z_{2}^{2}}[dz_{2}|d\theta_{2}]),

Hence, if
(\delta_{0,1}+\tau_{1}\delta_{1},\delta_{0,2}+\tau_{1}\delta_{2})(\theta_{1},0)\in\omega_{X/S}
and
\delta_{1}(\theta_{1})=\eta_{1}
, then we would get

(\eta_{1},\frac{\theta_{2}}{z_{2}^{2}}[dz_{2}|d\theta_{2}])\in\omega_{X/S},

with
\eta_{1}
regular on
U_{1}
, which is impossible by the above description of
\omega_{X/S}
.

Case of NS node, even deformations
.

In this case we work with the first-order deformation

z_{1}z_{2}=0,\ \ z_{1}\theta_{2}=t_{1}\theta_{1},\ \ z_{2}\theta_{1}=t_{2}\theta_{2},\ \ \theta_{1}\theta_{2}=0.

We have to check that the equation
t_{1}+t_{2}=0
on
S
is necessary in order for this to be in
t_{F}
.

The generator
s_{1}
(resp.,
s_{2}
) of
\omega_{X_{0}/S}
lifts to

\widetilde{s}_{1}=([dz_{1}|d\theta_{1}],-t_{1}\frac{1}{z_{2}}[dz_{2}|d\theta_{2}])\ (\text{resp., }\widetilde{s}_{2}=(-t_{2}\frac{1}{z_{1}}[dz_{1}|d\theta_{1}],[dz_{2}|d\theta_{2}])),

while the generator
s
lifts to the same pair on
X
that we denote
\widetilde{s}
.

The derivations
\delta_{0,i}:{\mathcal{O}}_{U_{i}}\to\omega_{U_{i}/S}
, for
i=1,2
, is still given by the formula (
3.2
).
We have

(\delta_{0,1},\delta_{0,2})(\theta_{1})=(\delta_{0,1},\delta_{0,2})(\theta_{1},t_{2}\frac{\theta_{2}}{z_{2}})=([dz_{1}|d\theta_{1}],t_{2}\frac{1}{z_{2}}[dz_{2}|d\theta_{2}])=\widetilde{s}_{1}+(t_{1}+t_{2})(0,\frac{1}{z_{2}}[dz_{2}|d\theta_{2}]),

Suppose
(\delta_{0,1}+t_{1}\delta_{1}+t_{2}\delta^{\prime}_{1},\delta_{0,2}+t_{1}\delta_{2}+t_{2}\delta^{\prime}_{2})(\theta_{1})\in\omega_{X/S}
. We have

(t_{1}\delta_{1},t_{1}\delta_{2})(\theta_{1})=(t_{1}\delta_{1}(\theta_{1}),t_{1}\delta_{2}(t_{2}\frac{\theta_{2}}{z_{2}}))=(t_{1}\eta,0),\ \ (t_{2}\delta^{\prime}_{1},t_{2}\delta^{\prime}_{2})(\theta_{1})=(t_{2}\eta^{\prime},0),

where we used the relations
t_{1}t_{2}=t_{2}^{2}=0
.
Hence, we get

((t_{1}\eta+t_{2}\eta^{\prime},(t_{1}+t_{2})\frac{1}{z_{2}}[dz_{2}|d\theta_{2}])\in\omega_{X/S},

with
\eta,\eta^{\prime}
regular on
U_{1}
, which is impossible unless

t_{1}+t_{2}=0
on
S
.
∎

Corollary 3.4
.

The functors of deformations of both types of nodes are smooth.

Proof.

Indeed, the functor
G
prorepresentable by
\C[\![t]\!]
is smooth. Since the morphism of deformation functors
G\to F
is smooth (where
F
is our deformation functor), this implies that
F
is smooth.
∎

The result on formal deformations implies in a standard way (essentially via Artin approximation techniques) the following étale local description of neighborhoods of singular points in families of prestable supercurves.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
X\to S
be a prestable supercurve, where
S
is of finite type over
\C
. Then for any node
q
in a fiber
X_{s}
over
s\in S
, there exists an étale neighborhood
V
of
s
in
S
,
such that for each preimage
q^{\prime}
of
q
in
X_{V}
, there exists a morphism
t:V\to{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
, such that étale locally near
q^{\prime}
the family
X_{V}\to V
is identified with the pull-back under
f
of the standard NS-node or Ramond-node deformation over
{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
.

Proof.

This is proved in
[
27
, Prop. 7.10]
. The key idea (also used in Artin approximation) is to use a presentation of the formal completion as an inductive
limit of smooth algebras given by Popescu’s theorem.
∎

3.3.
Sheaf of infinitesimal automorphisms

For a moment let us consider only supercurves over the point base
\operatorname{Spec}(\C)
.

Definition 3.6
.

We define the
\Z_{2}
-graded sheaf of infinitesimal automorphisms
{\mathcal{A}}
of any geometric structure of the form (superscheme over
\C

plus extra structure) as follows. The even part
{\mathcal{A}}^{+}
is given by automorphisms of the trivial family of these structures over
\C[\epsilon]/(\epsilon^{2})
,
where
\epsilon
is even, trivial modulo
\epsilon
. Similarly, the odd part
{\mathcal{A}}^{-}
is given by automorphisms of the trivial family over
\C[\tau]/(\tau^{2})
, where

\tau
is odd, trivial modulo
\tau
. For example, the sheaf of infinitesimal automorphisms of a superscheme is exactly the tangent sheaf.

For a stable supercurve
(X,P_{\bullet},R_{\bullet})
over
\C
, we denote by

{\mathcal{A}}_{X,P_{\bullet},R_{\bullet}}\subset{\mathcal{T}}_{X}

the sheaf of its infinitesimal automorphisms (where
{\mathcal{T}}_{X}
is the sheaf of super-derivations from
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
to
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
).
Note that the space of locally trivial
infinitesimal deformations of
(X,P_{\bullet},R_{\bullet})
is
H^{1}(X,{\mathcal{A}}_{X,P_{\bullet},R_{\bullet}})
.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
(X,P_{\bullet},R_{\bullet})
be a stable supercurve,
j:U\hookrightarrow X
a smooth locus. Then

{\mathcal{A}}_{(X,P_{\bullet},R_{\bullet})}
is identified with the subsheaf of
j_{*}{\mathcal{A}}_{U,P_{\bullet},R_{\bullet}}
consisting of
those derivations of
{\mathcal{O}}_{U}
that send
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
to
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
.

Proof.

Note that
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
is a subsheaf in
j_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{U}
, hence the sheaf
{\mathcal{T}}_{X}
of derivations from
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
to
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
,
can be identified with the subsheaf in
j_{*}{\mathcal{T}}_{U}
consisting of derivations of
{\mathcal{O}}_{U}
sending
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
to
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
.
Now the assertion follows from Lemma
2.7
(applied to automorphisms of
X\times\operatorname{Spec}(\C[\epsilon]/(\epsilon^{2}))
and
X\times\operatorname{Spec}(\C[\tau]/(\tau^{2}))
).
∎

Definition 3.8
.

Now let us consider a stable supercurve with punctures

(X,P_{\bullet},R_{\bullet})
over any base
S
.
We define

{\mathcal{A}}_{X/S}={\mathcal{A}}_{(X,P_{\bullet},R_{\bullet})/S}\subset{\mathcal{T}}_{X/S}

as the subsheaf consisting of derivations
v
in
{\mathcal{T}}_{X/S}
preserving the punctures and preserving the distribution
{\mathcal{D}}
over the smooth locus.
We still call
{\mathcal{A}}_{X/S}
the sheaf of infinitesimal automorphisms.

In the case
S=\operatorname{Spec}(\C)
this agrees with our previous definition. Indeed this follows easily from Lemma
3.7
which states
that
{\mathcal{A}}_{X}
is the intersection of
{\mathcal{T}}_{X}
with
j_{*}{\mathcal{A}}_{U}
in
j_{*}{\mathcal{T}}_{U}
.
Note however that the formation of
{\mathcal{A}}_{X/S}
is not compatible with the base change in general.

In the smooth case we have the following useful identification of the sheaf of infinitesimal automorphisms over an arbtrirary (not necessarily even) base.

Proposition 3.9
.

Let
(X,P_{\bullet},R_{\bullet})\to S
be a smooth supercurve with punctures, where
(P_{i})_{i\in I}
are NS punctures
and
(R_{j})_{j\in J}
are Ramond punctures.
Then one has a natural isomorphism

{\mathcal{A}}_{(X,P_{\bullet},R_{\bullet})/S}\simeq{\mathcal{T}}^{sc}_{(X,P_{\bullet})/S}\simeq\omega_{X/S}^{-2}(-\sum_{i\in I}D_{i}-2\sum_{j\in J}R_{j}),

where
D_{i}\subset X
is a divisor associated with the NS puncture
P_{i}
(see Sec.
2.5
).

Proof.

The first isomorphism corresponds to the fact that the Ramond punctures are recovered from the distribution
{\mathcal{D}}\subset{\mathcal{T}}_{X/S}
as the vanishing divisor
of the
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-linear map

{\mathcal{D}}^{\otimes 2}\to{\mathcal{T}}_{X/S}/{\mathcal{D}}

induced by the Lie bracket. Thus, if a relative derivation on
X/S
preserves
{\mathcal{D}}
and the NS punctures then it also preserves the Ramond punctures.

Next, we observe that there is a natural isomorphism

{\mathcal{T}}_{X/S}/{\mathcal{D}}\simeq{\mathcal{D}}^{2}(\sum_{j}R_{j})\simeq\omega_{X/S}^{-2}(-\sum_{j}R_{j}).

(see (
2.2
) and (
2.3
)). It remains to check that the natural map

{\mathcal{T}}^{sc}_{(X,P_{\bullet})/S}\to{\mathcal{T}}_{X/S}/{\mathcal{D}}
induces an isomorphism

{\mathcal{T}}^{sc}_{(X,P_{\bullet})/S}\rTo{\sim}({\mathcal{T}}_{X/S}/{\mathcal{D}})(-\sum_{i\in I}D_{i}-\sum_{j\in J}R_{j}).

This is a local question which is well known away from the punctures. Near the NS puncture this is Corollary
2.15
, while near the Ramond puncture
this is proved in
[
6
, Prop. 3.12]
using standard coordinates (see Lemma
2.10
).
∎

In the case when the base is a point it is useful to rewrite the result in terms of the corresponding spin curve.

Corollary 3.10
.

Let
(X,P_{\bullet},R_{\bullet})
be a smooth supercurve over
\C
with the underlying spin curve
(C,L,p_{\bullet},r_{\bullet})
, where

L^{2}\simeq\omega_{C}(\sum r_{j})
. Then

{\mathcal{A}}_{X,P_{\bullet},R_{\bullet}}^{+}\simeq{\mathcal{T}}_{C}(-\sum p_{i}-\sum r_{j}),\ \ {\mathcal{A}}_{X,P_{\bullet},R_{\bullet}}^{-}\simeq{\mathcal{T}}_{C}\otimes L(-\sum p_{i}-\sum r_{j}).

Proof.

Let
\pi:X\to C
be the projection. We have
\omega_{X}\simeq\pi^{*}L(-\sum r_{j})
(see Lemma
2.6
), so

\omega_{X}^{-2}\simeq\pi^{*}L^{-2}(2\sum r_{j})\simeq\pi^{*}{\mathcal{T}}_{C}(\sum r_{j}).

Hence,

\omega_{X}^{-2}(-\sum p_{i}-2\sum r_{j})\simeq\pi^{*}{\mathcal{T}}_{C}(-\sum p_{i}-\sum r_{j})={\mathcal{O}}_{X}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{C}}{\mathcal{T}}_{C}(-\sum p_{i}-\sum r_{j}).

Considering even and odd parts we get the result.
∎

3.4.
Sheaf of infinitesimal automorphisms for stable supercurves

The following local analysis of the sheaf
{\mathcal{A}}_{X,P_{\bullet},R_{\bullet}}
is from
[
13
]
(we corrected a misprint in the case of
the Ramond node).

Ramond node
.

Locally near such a node, we have
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}={\mathcal{O}}_{C}\oplus L
, where
L
is locally free,
L^{\otimes 2}\simeq\omega_{C}
,
and

{\mathcal{A}}_{X}={\mathcal{T}}_{C}\oplus L\otimes\omega_{C}^{-1}.

NS node
.

Let
C=B_{1}\cup B_{2}
be the two branches. Each
B_{i}
is equipped with a square root
L_{i}

of
\omega_{B_{i}}
.
Then
{\mathcal{A}}_{X}={\mathcal{A}}_{1}\oplus{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
, with

{\mathcal{A}}_{i}={\mathcal{T}}_{B_{i}}(-q_{i})\oplus L_{i}\otimes{\mathcal{T}}_{B_{i}}(-q_{i})

We can also determine the sheaf of infinitesimal automorphisms globally and
check the absence of infinitesimal automorphisms for stable supercurves by the standard count
(this is done in
[
13
]
for the case of supercurves without punctures).

Proposition 3.11
.

Let
(X,P_{\bullet},R_{\bullet})
be a stable supercurve over
\C
with the underlying stable spin curve
(C,L,p_{\bullet},r_{\bullet})
.
Let
\rho:\widetilde{C}\to C
be the normalization with the induced spin structure
\widetilde{L}

(see Sec.
2.6
), and let
(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{P}_{\bullet},\widetilde{R}_{\bullet})
be the corresponding smooth supercurve with punctures.

(i) One has natural a isomorphism

{\mathcal{A}}^{+}_{X,P_{\bullet},R_{\bullet}}\simeq\rho_{*}{\mathcal{A}}^{+}_{\widetilde{X},\widetilde{P}_{\bullet},\widetilde{R}_{\bullet}}\simeq{\mathcal{T}}_{C}(-\sum p_{i}-\sum r_{j})

and an exact sequence
