We will still have the equality
{\mathcal{A}}_{X,P,R}={\mathcal{A}}_{X,X_{0},P,R}
as in Lemma
6.5
, and the analogs of sequences (
6.14
)
and (
6.15
) are

0\to{\mathcal{A}}_{(X,P,R)/S}\to{\mathcal{A}}_{X,P,R}\to\pi^{-1}{\mathcal{T}}_{S,S_{0}}\to 0,

0\to{\mathcal{A}}_{(X_{0},P_{0},R_{0})/S}\to{\mathcal{A}}_{X_{0},P_{0},R_{0}}\to\pi_{0}^{-1}{\mathcal{T}}_{S_{0}}\to 0.

Indeed, the only extra statement is the surjectivity of the right arrows near the punctures, which follows from the standard local description of the punctures
(see Sec.
2.4
).

Recall also that by Theorem
4.4
, we have an isomorphism

{\mathcal{A}}_{(X,P_{\bullet},R_{\bullet})/S}\simeq{\mathcal{L}}(X,P_{\bullet},R_{\bullet}):=\omega_{X/S}^{-2}(-\sum_{i\in I}D_{i}-2\sum_{j\in J}R_{j}),

where
D_{i}\subset X
are the divisors associated with the NS nodes
P_{i}\subset X
, and
\omega_{X/S}^{-2}
is defined as in Theorem
4.1
.

The analog of Corollary
6.7
states that under the assumption that the map

\kappa_{x_{0}}:T_{s_{0}}S_{0}\to H^{1}(X_{s_{0}},{\mathcal{A}}_{X_{s_{0}},P_{s_{0}},R_{s_{0}}})

is injective and the superdimension of
S
is
3g-3|2g-2
, the Kodaira-Spencer map induces an isomorphism of line bundles on
S
,

\operatorname{Ber}({\mathcal{T}}_{S})\rTo{\sim}\operatorname{Ber}(R^{1}\pi_{*}{\mathcal{L}}(X,P_{\bullet},R_{\bullet}))(S_{0}).

7.
The boundary divisor

In this section we discuss the definition of the boundary of our compactification as an effective Cartier divisor. We also study the NS and the Ramond components
of the boundary divisor.

7.1.
Modified sheaf of differentials

Let
\pi:X\to S
be a family of stable supercurves such that the map from
S
to the deformation space of each node is smooth.
Let
j:U\to X
be the embedding of the smooth locus. We want to study the sheaf
j_{*}\Omega_{U/S}
and the map

\Omega_{X/S}\to j_{*}\Omega_{U/S}.

Since
U/S
is a smooth supercurve, we have an exact sequence

0\to\omega^{2}_{U/S}\rTo{\kappa}\Omega_{U/S}\rTo{\delta}\omega_{U/S}\to 0

where in standard coordinates

\delta(dz)=\theta[dz|d\theta],\ \ \delta(d\theta)=[dz|d\theta],

\kappa([dz|d\theta]^{\otimes 2})=dz-\theta d\theta.

Applying the functor
j_{*}
, we get an exact sequence

0\to\omega^{2}_{X/S}\rTo{\kappa}j_{*}\Omega_{U/S}\rTo{j_{*}\delta}\omega_{X/S},

where
\omega^{2}_{X/S}=j_{*}\omega_{U/S}
is a line bundle on
X
(see Theorem
4.1
).

Lemma 7.1
.

The map
j_{*}\delta
is surjective, so we have an exact sequence

0\to\omega^{2}_{X/S}\rTo{\kappa}j_{*}\Omega_{U/S}\rTo{j_{*}\delta}\omega_{X/S}\to 0

(7.1)

Proof.

We know that
j_{*}\delta
is surjective over
U
, so it is enough to check that it is surjective near the nodes.

Near a Ramond node, the map
\delta:\Omega_{X/S}\to\omega_{X/S}=j_{*}\omega_{U/S}
is surjective. Since it factors through

j_{*}\Omega_{U/S}
, we see that
j_{*}\delta
is surjective near a Ramond node.

To check surjectivity near an NS-node, we can work étale locally and apply Lemma
3.5
.
Hence, it remains to make a computation for the standard deformation of the NS-node.
Recall the generators
s_{1},s_{2},s_{0}
of
\omega_{X/S}
(see Lemma
6.4
).
We know that
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
are in the image of
\delta:\Omega_{X/S}\to\omega_{X/S}
.
On the other hand, the sections
dz_{1}/z_{1}
and
-dz_{2}/z_{2}
of
\Omega_{U/S}
over
z_{1}\neq 0
and
z_{2}\neq 0
glue into a global section of

j_{*}\Omega_{U/S}
, which maps to
s_{0}
under
j_{*}\delta
.
∎

Now let us consider a family of stable supercurves
X/S
over a smooth affine base
S
with a smooth map
t:S\to{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
, such that over
S_{0}=(t=0)\subset S
,
we have a relative NS node
q:S_{0}\to X_{0}=S_{0}\times_{S}X
, so that
X\setminus q(S_{0})
is smooth over
S
,
the completion of
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
along
q(S_{0})
is given by the standard generators and relations
(
6.11
), and the derivation
\delta
is given by the standard formula.

Lemma 7.2
.

(i) In the above situation, étale locally along
q(S_{0})
, the sheaf
j_{*}\Omega_{U/S}
is generated as an
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-module by global sections

e=\frac{dz_{1}}{z_{1}}=-\frac{dz_{2}}{z_{2}},\ \ d\theta_{1},\ \ d\theta_{2},\ \ f=\frac{\theta_{1}d\theta_{1}}{z_{1}}=-\frac{\theta_{2}d\theta_{2}}{z_{2}},

with defining relations

\displaystyle td\theta_{1}-z_{1}d\theta_{2}=t\theta_{1}e,\ \ z_{2}d\theta_{1}+td\theta_{2}=-t\theta_{2}e,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \

\displaystyle tf=\theta_{2}d\theta_{1}=\theta_{1}d\theta_{2},\ \ z_{1}f=\theta_{1}d\theta_{1},\ \ z_{2}f=-\theta_{2}d\theta_{2},\ \ \theta_{1}f=\theta_{2}f=0.

(7.2)

(ii) We have an exact sequence on
X_{0}
,

0\to{\mathcal{K}}\to\Omega_{X_{0}/S_{0}}\to j_{*}\Omega_{U/S}|_{X_{0}}\to{\mathcal{C}}_{0}\to 0

where
{\mathcal{K}}
has an
{\mathcal{O}}_{S_{0}}
-basis

z_{1}dz_{2}=-z_{2}dz_{1},\ \ z_{1}d\theta_{2}=-\theta_{2}dz_{1},\ \ z_{2}d\theta_{1}=-\theta_{1}dz_{2},\ \ \theta_{1}d\theta_{2}=\theta_{2}d\theta_{1},

(7.3)

and
{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
has an
{\mathcal{O}}_{S_{0}}
-basis the images of

e,\ \ f,\ \ \theta_{1}e,\ \ \theta_{2}e.

(7.4)

Furthermore, the map of
{\mathcal{O}}_{S_{0}}
-modules
\pi_{*}{\mathcal{K}}\to\pi_{*}\Omega_{X_{0}/S_{0}}
is an embedding of a direct summand.

Proof.

(i) Étale locally we can think of sections of
j_{*}\Omega_{U/S}
as compatible sections of
\Omega_{U_{1}/S}
and
\Omega_{U_{2}/S}
, where
U_{i}
is the open subset
where
z_{i}
is invertible. Thus,
e
is a well defined section of
j_{*}\Omega_{U/S}
.
Using relations (
6.11
), we get

z_{2}d\theta_{1}=-td\theta_{2}-\theta_{1}dz_{2},

so over
U_{1}\cap U_{2}
,

\frac{\theta_{1}}{z_{1}}d\theta_{1}=\frac{\theta_{2}}{t}(-\frac{t}{z_{2}}d\theta_{2})=-\frac{\theta_{2}}{z_{2}}d\theta_{2},

so
f
is a well defined section of
j_{*}\Omega_{U/S}
.

Let us denote by
{\mathcal{F}}
the
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-module given by generators
e
,
f
,
d\theta_{1}
and
d\theta_{2}
and relations (
7.2
)
It is straightforward to check (by inverting
z_{1}
or
z_{2}
) that these relations are satisfied in
j_{*}\Omega_{U/S}
, so we have a well defined morphism

{\mathcal{F}}\to j_{*}\Omega_{U/S}
.
The map
\kappa:\omega^{2}_{X/S}\to j_{*}\Omega_{U/S}
sends a generator to
e-f
. Thus, we
we have a commutative diagram with exact rows

\begin{diagram}

(7.5)

One can check using Gröbner basis technique that

z_{1}^{n}e,\ \ z_{1}^{n}\theta_{1}e,\ \ z_{1}^{n}d\theta_{1},\ \ z_{1}^{n}\theta_{1}d\theta_{1},\ \ z_{2}^{n}e,\ \ z_{2}^{n}\theta_{2}e,\ \ z_{2}^{n}d\theta_{2},\ \ z_{2}^{n}\theta_{2}d\theta_{2},\ \ f,

where
n\geq 0
, is an
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
-basis of
{\mathcal{F}}
. This easily implies
that the map
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\rTo{e-f}{\mathcal{F}}
is injective (by looking at the coefficients of
e
).

The quotient
{\mathcal{F}}/{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(e-f)
is generated by
e,d\theta_{1},d\theta_{2}
subject to relations

td\theta_{1}-z_{1}d\theta_{2}=0,\ \ z_{2}d\theta_{1}+td\theta_{2}=0,

te=\theta_{2}d\theta_{1}=\theta_{1}d\theta_{2},\ \ z_{1}e=\theta_{1}d\theta_{1},\ \ z_{2}e=-\theta_{2}d\theta_{2},\ \ \theta_{1}e=\theta_{2}e=0.

Note that

\delta(d\theta_{1})=s_{1},\ \ \delta(d\theta_{2})=s_{2},\ \ \delta(e)=s_{0}.

Comparing these with generators and relations of
\omega_{X/S}
(see Lemma
6.4
)
we deduce that the map
{\mathcal{F}}/{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(e-f)\to\omega_{X/S}
is an isomorphism.
Now the fact that
{\mathcal{F}}\to j_{*}\Omega_{U/S}
is an isomorphism follows from the diagram (
7.5
).

(ii) From (i) we see that
j_{*}\Omega_{U/S}
is a coherent sheaf (since this can be checked étale locally along
q(S_{0})
).
Hence, the kernel
{\mathcal{K}}
and the cokernel
{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
of the map
\Omega_{X_{0}/S_{0}}\to j_{*}\Omega_{U/S}|_{X_{0}}
are coherent sheaves supported at the node.
Also, we get an explicit description of the completion of
j_{*}\Omega_{U/S}|_{X_{0}}
at the node by generators and relations.
Taking the quotient by the image of
\Omega_{X_{0}/S}
,
we get that
{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
(which coincides with its completion at the node) is generated by the images
(\overline{e},\overline{f})
of
(e,f)
, with the defining relations

z_{1}\overline{e}=0,\ \ z_{2}\overline{e}=0,\ \ z_{1}\overline{f}=0,\ \ z_{2}\overline{f}=0,\ \ \theta_{1}\overline{f}=0,\ \ \theta_{2}\overline{f}=0.

This implies the assertion that the elements (
7.4
) constitute an
{\mathcal{O}}_{S_{0}}
-basis of
{\mathcal{C}}
.

The kernel
{\mathcal{K}}
coincides with the kernel of the restriction
\Omega_{X_{0}/S_{0}}\to\Omega_{X_{1}/S_{0}}\oplus\Omega_{X_{2}/S_{0}}
. The four elements (
7.3
)
can be extended to an
{\mathcal{O}}_{S_{0}}
-basis of
\Omega_{X_{0}/S_{0}}
: we have to add

z_{i}^{n}dz_{i},\ z_{i}^{n}\theta_{i}dz_{i},\ z_{i}^{n}d\theta_{i},\ z_{i}^{n}\theta_{i}d\theta_{i},

where
i=1,2
,
n\geq 0
. Since the latter elements project to independent elements of
\Omega_{X_{1}/S_{0}}\oplus\Omega_{X_{2}/S_{0}}
,
the assertion follows.
∎

Next let us consider the case of a Ramond node, i.e., consider similar data
(X/S,t:S\to{\mathbb{A}}^{1},q:S_{0}\to X_{0})
, such that
q(S_{0})
is
a relative Ramond node, and étale locally along
q(S_{0})
,
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
is given by the standard generators
z_{1},z_{2},\theta
subject
to
z_{1}z_{2}=t
, and with the distribution generated by
\partial_{\theta}+\theta z_{i}\partial_{z_{i}}
over
z_{i}\neq 0
.

Lemma 7.3
.

(i) In the above situation the sheaf
j_{*}\Omega_{U/S}
is freely generated in a formal neighborhood of
q(S_{0})
, as an
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-module, by global sections

e=\frac{dz_{1}}{z_{1}}=-\frac{dz_{2}}{z_{2}},\ \ d\theta.

(ii) We have an exact sequence on
X_{0}
,

0\to{\mathcal{K}}\to\Omega_{X_{0}/S_{0}}\to j_{*}\Omega_{U/S}|_{X_{0}}\to{\mathcal{C}}_{0}\to 0

where
{\mathcal{K}}\simeq q_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{S_{0}}
is generated by
z_{1}dz_{2}=-z_{2}dz_{1}

and
{\mathcal{C}}_{0}\simeq q_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{S_{0}}
is generated by the image of
e
.
Furthermore, the map of
{\mathcal{O}}_{S_{0}}
-modules
\pi_{*}{\mathcal{K}}\to\pi_{*}\Omega_{X_{0}/S_{0}}
is an embedding of a direct summand.

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of Lemma
7.2
but is much easier since both
\omega_{X/S}
and
\omega_{X/S}^{2}
are locally free near
the Ramond node. Namely,
\omega_{X/S}
is freely generated by the section glued from
\frac{1}{z_{1}}[dz_{1}|d\theta]
and
-\frac{1}{z_{2}}[dz_{2}|d\theta]
,
The map
\kappa
sends a generator of
\omega_{X/S}^{2}
to
e-\theta d\theta
.
Since
\delta
sends
d\theta
to a generator of
\omega_{X/S}
, we deduce that
e
and
d\theta
freely generate
j_{*}\Omega_{U/S}
over
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
.

The map
\Omega_{X_{0}/S_{0}}\to j_{*}\Omega_{U/S}|_{X_{0}}
sends
dz_{1}
to
z_{1}e
,
dz_{2}

to
-z_{2}e
, and
d\theta
to
d\theta
. This easily implies that its kernel is generated by
z_{2}dz_{1}=-z_{1}dz_{2}
, while its cokernel is generate by the image of
e
.
∎

7.2.
Boundary Cartier divisor

The boundary divisor of the compactified moduli superspace has codimension
1|0
, however, since the supermoduli space is not reduced,
the structure of the Cartier divisor on the boundary is not automatically given. We claim however that there is a natural such structure.

Let
\pi:X\to S
be a family of stable supercurves, inducing a surjection to the miniversal space of deformations of each node.
We are going to study the natural
2
-term complex

[\Omega_{X/S}\to j_{*}\Omega_{U/S}]

placed in degrees
-1
and
0
, where
j:U\to X
is the open embedding of the complement to the nodes.
Note that this complex is acyclic over
U
. Furthermore, both terms are flat over
S
, so
the line bundle
\operatorname{Ber}\pi_{*}[\Omega_{X/S}\to j_{*}\Omega_{U/S}]
is well defined. Similarly
to the theory in the even case (see
[
24
]
), we can define a canonical trivialization of this line bundle away from the locus of nodal supercurves.

Proposition 7.4
.

The canonical trivialization
c
of
{\mathcal{B}}:=\operatorname{Ber}\pi_{*}[\Omega_{X/S}\to j_{*}\Omega_{U/S}]
away from the nodal locus extends to a regular section of
{\mathcal{B}}
on
S
that has form
t\cdot b
near
a point corresponding to a stable supercurve with one NS or Ramond node, for some trivializing local section
b
of
{\mathcal{B}}
and some local function
t
whose reduction
modulo nilpotents gives an equation of the corresponding reduced divisor.

Proof.

We can work in an étale neighborhood of a point on
S
corresponding to a stable supercurve with one node.
Let us first consider the case of an NS node.
We can choose a sufficiently positive relative Cartier divisor
D
(disjoint from the node) such that
\pi_{*}[\Omega_{X/S}\to j_{*}\Omega_{U/S}]
will be represented by
the compex of supervector bundles

\pi_{*}(\Omega_{X/S}(D))\rTo{\iota}\pi_{*}(j_{*}\Omega_{U/S}(D)).

To understand this map, we first restrict it to
X_{0}
.
We have an exact sequence on
S
,

0\to\pi_{*}{\mathcal{K}}\to\pi_{*}(\Omega_{X_{0}/S_{0}}(D))\to\pi_{*}(j_{*}\Omega_{U/S}(D)|_{S_{0}})\to\pi_{*}{\mathcal{C}}_{0}\to 0,

where both
\pi_{*}{\mathcal{K}}
and
\pi_{*}{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
are locally free of rank
2|2
.
Furthermore,
\pi_{*}{\mathcal{K}}
embeds into
\pi_{*}(\Omega_{X_{0}/S_{0}}(D))
as a subbundle, and we have an
{\mathcal{O}}_{S_{0}}
-basis (
7.3
) of

\pi_{*}{\mathcal{K}}
. Note that the elements of this basis are defined in terms of the coordinates which only exist locally near the node. However,
they are killed by the ideal
I_{q}
of the node, so they can be viewed as sections of
\pi_{*}\Omega_{X_{0}/S_{0}}(D)
.

We can choose liftings of the
{\mathcal{O}}_{S_{0}}
-basis (
7.3
) in
\pi_{*}{\mathcal{K}}
to
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
-independent sections
b_{1},b_{2},b_{3},b_{4}
in
\pi_{*}(\Omega_{X/S}(D))
, so
that

b_{1}\equiv z_{1}dz_{2}\mod(t),\ b_{2}\equiv\theta_{2}dz_{1}\mod(t),\ b_{3}\equiv\theta_{1}dz_{2}\mod(t),\ b_{4}\equiv\theta_{2}d\theta_{1}\mod(t).

Then we can extend it to a basis
(b_{1},\ldots,b_{n})
of
\pi_{*}(\Omega_{X/S}(D))
so that the elements
(\iota(b_{5}),\ldots,\iota(b_{n}))

form a basis of a free
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
-submodule in
\pi_{*}(j_{*}\Omega_{U/S}(D))
, which projects modulo
(t)
to a basis of the image of

\pi_{*}\Omega_{X_{0}/S_{0}}(D)
.

Next, we adjust our choice of
b_{1}
so that

b_{1}\equiv z_{1}dz_{2}\mod(t^{2}).

Here the right-hand side can be viewed as a section of

\pi_{*}(\Omega_{X/S}(D))/(t^{2})=\pi_{*}(\Omega_{X_{0}^{(1)}/S_{0}^{(1)}}(D)),

where we consider the base change
X_{0}^{(1)}\to S_{0}^{(1)}
, where
S_{0}^{(1)}\subset S
is given by the ideal
t^{2}
.
Namely, we observe that near the node one has
I_{q}^{2}\subset(z_{1},z_{2})
, which implies that
z_{1}dz_{2}=-z_{2}dz_{1}
is killed by
I_{q}^{2}
on
X_{0}^{(1)}

(due to the relation
z_{1}z_{2}=-t^{2}
).
Thus, we can lift
z_{1}dz_{2}\mod(t^{2})
to a section
b_{1}\in\pi_{*}(\Omega_{X/S}(D))
.

Now let us analyse the images of
b_{1},\ldots,b_{4}
under
\iota
.
We have
\iota(b_{i})=tc_{i}
for some
c_{i}\in\pi_{*}(j_{*}\Omega_{U/S}(D))
, for
i=2,3,4
.
In the case of
b_{1}
we know that
\iota(b_{1})=t^{2}c_{1}
for some
c_{1}\in\pi_{*}(j_{*}\Omega_{U/S}(D))
.

Furthermore, the restriction of
\iota(b_{i})
to a formal neighborhood
\hat{X}
of the node is the image
of the restriction of
b_{i}
to this formal neighborhood under the similar map for
\hat{X}
.
Since
b_{1}
restricts to
z_{1}dz_{2}+t^{2}x
, it maps to

-z_{1}z_{2}e+t^{2}x=t^{2}(e+x),

where
x
comes from
\Omega_{\hat{X}/S}
.
Hence,

c_{1}\equiv e\mod\Omega_{\hat{X}/S}.

Similarly, we compute

\theta_{2}dz_{1}=\theta_{2}z_{1}e=t\theta_{1}e,

\theta_{1}dz_{2}=-\theta_{1}z_{2}e=t\theta_{2}e,

\theta_{2}d\theta_{1}=tf.

Hence, we get

c_{2}\equiv\theta_{1}e\mod\Omega_{\hat{X}/S},\ \ c_{3}\equiv\theta_{2}e\mod\Omega_{\hat{X}/S},\ \ c_{4}\equiv f\mod\Omega_{\hat{X}/S}.

It follows that
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3},c_{4}
project to an
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
-basis of
\pi_{*}{\mathcal{C}}_{0}
.
Hence,
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3},c_{4}
are linearly indpendent over
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
, and
can be extended to an
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
-basis
(c_{1},\ldots,c_{n})
of
\pi_{*}(j_{*}\Omega_{U/S}(D))
by
c_{i}=\iota(b_{i})
for
i>4
.
Computing the Berezinian in this basis we get
t
.

The case of Ramond node is considered in a similar way using Lemma
7.3
.
In this case we can choose bases
(b_{1},\ldots,b_{n})
of
\pi_{*}(\Omega_{X/S}(D))
and
(c_{1},\ldots,c_{n})
of
\pi_{*}(j_{*}\Omega_{U/S}(D))
, such that

b_{1}\equiv z_{1}dz_{2}\mod(t^{2})
,
\iota(b_{1})=-tc_{1}
(since
z_{1}dz_{2}=-z_{1}z_{2}e=-te
in
j_{*}\Omega_{U/S}(D)
), and
\iota(b_{i})=c_{i}
for
i>1
.
This shows that the Berezinian is
t
.
∎

The above Proposition gives a natural definition of the boundary divisor in the moduli space of stable supercurves
\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}
as an effective Cartier divisor.
More precisely, let
k:{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}
be the complement to the locus of stable supercurves with more than
1
node. Since
the even codimension of the latter locus is
>1
and
\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}
is smooth, for any vector bundle
{\mathcal{V}}
over
\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}
, the natural map

{\mathcal{V}}\to k_{*}k^{*}{\mathcal{V}}
is an isomorphism.
Let us consider the line bundle

{\mathcal{L}}:=\operatorname{Ber}\pi_{*}[\Omega_{X/\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}}\to j_{*}\Omega_{U/\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}}]

over
\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}
.
By Proposition
7.4
, the canonical trivialization
c
of
{\mathcal{L}}
over the smooth locus gives a regular global section of
k^{*}{\mathcal{L}}

and hence of
{\mathcal{L}}\simeq k_{*}k^{*}{\mathcal{L}}
.
We define the effective Cartier divisor
\Delta
to be the vanishing divisor of this global section.
Note that by definition, we have an isomorphism

{\mathcal{O}}(\Delta)\simeq{\mathcal{L}}\simeq\operatorname{Ber}R\pi_{*}(j_{*}\Omega_{U/S})\otimes\operatorname{Ber}^{-1}R\pi_{*}(\Omega_{X/S}).

(7.6)

The proof of Proposition
7.4
also yields the following characterization of the divisor
\Delta
.

Lemma 7.5
.

The effective Cartier divisor
\Delta
is a unique Cartier divisor supported on the locus of nodal supercurves with the following two properties:

•

for any stable supercurve
X_{0}
with a single NS node
q
there exists an étale neighborhood
S
of the corresponding
point
[X_{0}]
in the moduli space and an étale neighborhood
X_{S}
of

q
in the family
X\to S
induced by the universal family such that
{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{S}}
is generated over
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
by
z_{1},z_{2},\theta_{1},\theta_{2}
subject to relations
(
6.11
), where
t
is a local equation of
\Delta
on
S
;

•

for any stable supercurve
X_{0}
with a single Ramond node
q
there exists an étale neighborhood
S
of
[X_{0}]
in the moduli space and an étale neighborhood
X_{S}
of

q
in the family
X\to S
induced by the universal family such that
{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{S}}
is generated over
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
by
z_{1},z_{2},\theta
subject to the relation

z_{1}z_{2}=t
, where
t
is a local equation of
\Delta
on
S
;

Proof.

These conditions clearly characterize
\Delta\cap{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}
. To show uniqueness of an extension to
\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}
, we observe that if
{\mathcal{I}}\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}}
is
an invertible ideal sheaf then
{\mathcal{I}}
is identified with
k_{*}k^{*}{\mathcal{I}}\subset k_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}}={\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}}
.
∎

7.3.
Another definition of the boundary divisor and the normal crossing property

Let
X/\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}
be the universal stable supercurve.

Lemma 7.6
.

The natural morphism
{\mathcal{A}}_{X}\to\pi^{*}{\mathcal{T}}_{\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}}
of sheaves on
X

factors through a morphism
{\mathcal{A}}_{X}\to\pi^{-1}{\mathcal{T}}_{\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}}
.

Proof.

This follows the fact that
{\mathcal{A}}_{X}=j_{*}{\mathcal{A}}_{U}
, where
U\subset X
is the complement to the nodes (see the proof of Theorem
4.4
),
and from the corresponding statement for the smooth locus
U\to\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}
(see Eq. (
6.9
)).
∎

Let us consider the natural morphism

\pi_{*}({\mathcal{A}}_{X}/{\mathcal{A}}_{X/\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}})\to{\mathcal{T}}_{\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}}

(7.7)

induced by the map
{\mathcal{A}}_{X}\to\pi^{-1}{\mathcal{T}}_{\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}}
.

Proposition 7.7
.

The
{\mathcal{O}}_{\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}}
-module
\pi_{*}({\mathcal{A}}_{X}/{\mathcal{A}}_{X/\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}})
is locally free of rank
(3g-3|2g-2)
over
\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}
, and the Cartier divisor
associated with the Berezinian of the map (
7.7
) coincides with
\Delta
. Furthermore,
\Delta
is a normal crossing divisor.

Proof.

Note that (
7.7
) is an isomorphism over the locus of smooth supercurves.
By Lemma
6.5
, in an étale neighborhood
S
of a point
[X_{0}]
, where
X_{0}
has a single NS node, the image of the morphism

{\mathcal{A}}_{X}\to\pi^{-1}{\mathcal{T}}_{S}
is
\pi^{-1}{\mathcal{T}}_{S,S_{0}}
, where
S_{0}
is the divisor
(t)
.
Hence, in this case (
7.7
) is the embedding
{\mathcal{T}}_{B,B_{0}}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{T}}_{B}
, and the Berezinian of this morphism is
t
.
By Lemma
6.8
, the similar statement holds in a neighborhood of a curve with a single Ramond node.
This implies that
\pi_{*}({\mathcal{A}}_{X}/{\mathcal{A}}_{X/\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}})
is locally free over
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}\subset\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}
, and the divisor of the Berezinian of (
7.7
) over
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}
is

\Delta\cap{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}
.

Nex, let us study the situation near a point
[X_{0}]
of
\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}
, such that
[X_{0}]
has several nodes
q_{1},\ldots,q_{k}
.
Using the fact that the map from deformations of
[X_{0}]
to the product of deformation spaces of the nodes is smooth, we see that there exists
an étale neighborhood
B
of
[X_{0}]
, together with a smooth map
(t_{1},\ldots,t_{k}):B\to{\mathbb{A}}^{k}
such that the function
t_{i}
corresponds the induced deformation of
the node
q_{i}
.

Let us consider the normal crossing divisor
D=(t_{1}\ldots t_{k}=0)
in
B
, and let
{\mathcal{T}}_{B,D}\subset{\mathcal{T}}_{B}
be the subsheaf of derivations preserving
D
.
Note that
{\mathcal{T}}_{B,D}=\cap{\mathcal{T}}_{B,D_{i}}
where
D_{i}
is the divisor
t_{i}=0
.

Let us consider the restriction
\pi:X\to B
of the universal family and set

\overline{{\mathcal{A}}}_{X}:={\mathcal{A}}_{X}/{\mathcal{A}}_{X/B}\subset\pi^{*}{\mathcal{T}}_{B}.

Let
U_{i}
be an open neighborhood of
q_{i}
in
X
, and let
\pi_{i}=\pi|_{U_{i}}
. Then, as we have seen above,
we have

\overline{{\mathcal{A}}}_{X}|_{U_{i}}=\pi_{i}^{-1}{\mathcal{T}}_{B,D_{i}}.

Thus, for each
i=1,\ldots,k
, we have an inclusion

\pi_{*}(\overline{{\mathcal{A}}}_{X})\subset\pi_{i*}(\overline{{\mathcal{A}}}_{X}|_{U_{i}})={\mathcal{T}}_{B,D_{i}}\subset{\mathcal{T}}_{B}.

Hence, we deduce the inclusion

\pi_{*}(\overline{{\mathcal{A}}}_{X})\subset{\mathcal{T}}_{B,D}.

On the other hand, we claim that there is an inclusion

\pi^{-1}{\mathcal{T}}_{B,D}\subset\overline{{\mathcal{A}}}_{X}.

Indeed, it is enough to check this over each
U_{i}
. But then we have

\pi^{-1}{\mathcal{T}}_{B,D}|_{U_{i}}\subset\pi_{i}^{-1}{\mathcal{T}}_{B,D_{i}}=\overline{{\mathcal{A}}}_{X},

as required.
Hence, passing to
\pi_{*}(?)
, we derive the inclusion

{\mathcal{T}}_{B,D}=\pi_{*}\pi^{-1}{\mathcal{T}}_{B,D}\subset\pi_{*}\overline{{\mathcal{A}}}_{X}.

as claimed.

Thus, we get
\pi_{*}\overline{{\mathcal{A}}}_{X}={\mathcal{T}}_{B,D}
.
Hence,
\pi_{*}\overline{{\mathcal{A}}}_{X}
is locally free and the Berezinian of the morphism
{\mathcal{T}}_{B,D}\to{\mathcal{T}}_{B}
has the required form.
∎

7.4.
NS and Ramond boundary components as effective Cartier divisors

Recall that we denote by
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}\subset\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}
the open locus of stable supercurves with at most one node.
Note that
\Delta\cap{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}
is the disjoint union of two Cartier divisors, one supported on stable supercurves with one NS node and another supported on
those with a Ramond node. We want to extend these two divisors on
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}
to effective Cartier divisors
\Delta_{NS}
and
\Delta_{R}
on
\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}
such that

\Delta=\Delta_{NS}+\Delta_{R}
.

First, we are going to define the divisor
\Delta_{NS}
giving the NS component.
For this let us consider the structure map

\delta:\Omega_{X/\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}}\to\omega_{X/\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}}(R).

As we have seen before, it is surjective away from the nodes.
It is also surjective on Ramond nodes.
Let us consider the ideal sheaf on
\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}
,

{\mathcal{I}}_{\delta}:=\operatorname{Ann}\pi_{*}\operatorname{coker}(\delta),

supported on the locus of stable supercurves with at least one NS node.

Proposition 7.8
.

(i) The ideal sheaf
{\mathcal{I}}_{\delta}
defines an effective Cartier divisor
\Delta_{NS}
on
\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}
, which coincides with
\Delta

in a neighborhood of any point
[X_{0}]
corresponding to a stable supercurve
X_{0}
with only NS nodes.

(ii) There exists a unique effective Cartier divisor
\Delta_{R}
supported on the locus of stable supercurves with at least one Ramond node,
such that

\Delta=\Delta_{NS}+\Delta_{R}.

If
X_{0}
is a stable supercurve with NS nodes
q_{1},\ldots,q_{r}
and Ramond nodes
q_{r+1},\ldots,q_{k}
, then there exists an étale neighborhood
B
of

[X_{0}]
in
\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}
with a smooth morphism
t_{1},\ldots,t_{k}:B\to{\mathbb{A}}^{k}
such that
t_{1}\ldots t_{r}
is an equation of
\Delta_{NS}
and
t_{r+1}\ldots t_{k}
is an equation
of
\Delta_{R}
.

Proof.

Let
X_{0}
be a stable supercurve with NS nodes
q_{1},\ldots,q_{r}
and Ramond nodes
q_{r+1},\ldots,q_{k}
.
Consider an étale neighborhood
B
of
[X_{0}]
, equipped with a smooth map
(t_{1},\ldots,t_{k}):B\to{\mathbb{A}}^{k}
,
such that
t_{i}
gives the universal deformation of the node
q_{i}
.
Then the ideal of
\Delta
is generated by
t_{1}\ldots t_{k}
.

On the other hand, using the description of
\omega_{X/S}
by generators and relations (see Lemma
6.4
), we see that in the formal neighborhood of each NS node
q_{i}
,

\operatorname{coker}(\delta)
is generated by
s_{0}
subject to the relations

\theta_{1}s_{0}=\theta_{2}s_{0}=z_{1}s_{0}=z_{2}s_{0}=t_{i}s_{0}=0.

It is easy to deduce from this that the annihilator of
\pi_{*}\operatorname{coker}(\delta)
is generated by
t_{1}\ldots t_{r}
.
This implies all the assertions.
∎

7.5.
The NS node boundary components

We will use the following construction of
gluing two superschemes along a closed subscheme
.

Assume that
X
,
X^{\prime}
and
Y
are superschemes,
i:Y\to X
and
i:Y\to X^{\prime}
are closed embeddings.
We want to define a new superscheme
Z
by gluing
X
and
X^{\prime}
along
Y
. As a topological space we
can define
Z
to the usual gluing of the topological spaces of
X
and
X^{\prime}
along
Y
, so that we have
closed embeddings
j:X\to Z
,
j^{\prime}:X^{\prime}\to Z
, so that
k:=j_{1}\circ i=j_{2}\circ i
. Hence, we have two homomorphisms
of sheaves of rings

\phi:j_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\to k_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{Y},\ \ \phi^{\prime}:j^{\prime}_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{X^{\prime}}\to k_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{Y},

and we define
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
to be the subsheaf of
j_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\oplus j^{\prime}_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{X^{\prime}}
, namely, the preimage of the diagonal

k_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{Y}\subset k_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{Y}\oplus k_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{Y}
under
\phi\oplus\phi^{\prime}
.

We can apply this to gluing two stable supercurves along NS-punctures.

Suppose
X/S
and
X^{\prime}/S
is a pair of stable supercurves with NS-punctures
P\subset X
and
P^{\prime}\subset X^{\prime}
.
We have an isomorphism
P\simeq S\simeq P^{\prime}
, so we can define a new superscheme
Z/S
by gluing
X
and
X^{\prime}

along
P\simeq P^{\prime}
.

Lemma 7.9
.

The glued superscheme
Z/S
has a natural stable supercurve structure such that the derivation
\delta
on
Z
is defined as the composition

{\mathcal{O}}_{Z}\to j_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\oplus j^{\prime}_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{X^{\prime}}\rTo{(j_{*}\delta,j^{\prime}_{*}\delta^{\prime})}j_{*}\omega_{X/S}\oplus j^{\prime}_{*}\omega_{X^{\prime}/S}\to\omega_{Z/S}.

Proof.

If
U\subset X
,
U^{\prime}\subset X^{\prime}
are smooth loci, then
(U\setminus P)\sqcup(U^{\prime}\setminus P^{\prime})
is an open subset of
Z
, which is a smooth supercurve over
S
.
Next, we need to check that in the case when
S
is a point,
\delta^{-}
induces an isomorphism of
{\mathcal{O}}_{Z}^{-}
with
\omega_{Z}^{-}
.
Since the base is even, we have identifications

{\mathcal{O}}_{X}={\mathcal{O}}_{C}\oplus L,\ \ {\mathcal{O}}_{X^{\prime}}={\mathcal{O}}_{C^{\prime}}\oplus L^{\prime},

and the smooth marked points
P\subset C
,
P^{\prime}\subset C^{\prime}
, so that the embedding of
P
into
X
corresponds to the projection

{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{C}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{P}.

Note that
L
and
L^{\prime}
are generalized spin-structures, i.e., we have an isomorphism
L\rTo{\sim}\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}(L,\omega_{C})
(which corresponds to
\delta^{-}
on
X
),
and similarly, for
L^{\prime}
. Furthermore, we know that
L
(resp.,
L^{\prime}
) is locally free near
P
(resp.,
P^{\prime}
).

The glued superscheme
Z
has the underlying nodal curve
Z_{0}
, which is glued from
C
and
C^{\prime}
along
P\simeq P^{\prime}
,
and

{\mathcal{O}}_{Z}\simeq{\mathcal{O}}_{Z_{0}}\oplus j_{*}L\oplus j^{\prime}_{*}L^{\prime}.

It is well known that the natural map

j_{*}L\oplus j^{\prime}_{*}L^{\prime}\to\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}(j_{*}L\oplus j^{\prime}_{*}L^{\prime},\omega_{Z_{0}})

is an isomorphism, i.e.,
j_{*}L\oplus j^{\prime}_{*}L^{\prime}
is a generalized spin-structure.
It is easy to see that above map is precisely
\delta^{-}
, so this proves that
Z/S
is a stable supercurve.
∎

Let
\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}_{g;m,n}
denote the moduli superspace of stable supercurves of genus
g
with
m
NS marked points and
n
Ramond marked points.
Then we can apply the above gluing construction to the pair of families of stable supercurves over

\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}_{g_{1};m_{1}+1,n_{1}}\times\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}_{g_{2};m_{2}+1,n_{2}}
, pulled back from each factor and using the last NS puncture on each of them.
This leads to a morphism

\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}_{g_{1};m_{1}+1,n_{1}}\times\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}_{g_{2};m_{2}+1,n_{2}}\to\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}_{g_{1}+g_{2};m_{1}+m_{2},n_{1}+n_{2}}.

(7.8)

Similarly, if
X/S
is a stable supercurve with two disjoint NS-punctures
P,P^{\prime}\subset X
, then we can glue
P
with
P^{\prime}
and get a new stable supercurve with
a non-separating node. This leads to a morphism

\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}_{g;m+2,n}\to\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}_{g+1;m,n}

(7.9)

Lemma 7.10
.

Both morphisms (
7.8
) and (
7.9
) induce embeddings of codimension
1|0
on tangent spaces and factor
through the divisor
\Delta_{NS}
. Furthermore, near a stable supercurve with a single separating (resp., non-separating)
NS node, the divisor
\Delta_{NS}
coincides with the schematic image of (
7.8
) (resp., (
7.9
)).

Proof.

The idea is to use exact sequences (
3.9
).
Let
X
be a stable supercurve with punctures and a fixed NS-node
q\in X
, and let
\rho:\widetilde{X}\to X
be the normalization at
q
, equipped with the two NS punctures over
q
.
Then by Proposition
3.11
(i), we have

{\mathcal{A}}_{X}\simeq\rho_{*}{\mathcal{A}}_{\widetilde{X}}

(where we take into account all the punctures on both sides).
Hence, the natural map

H^{1}(\widetilde{X},{\mathcal{A}}_{\widetilde{X}})\to H^{1}(X,{\mathcal{A}}_{X})

is an isomorphism.

Let
q_{1},\ldots,q_{m}
be the nodes of
X
different from
q
. Then we have a morphism of exact sequences

{diagram}

in which the right vertical arrow is the natural inclusion of codimension
1|0
. Since the left vertical arrow is an isomorphism, the assertion about the
map of tangent spaces follows.

To check that our morphisms factor through
\Delta_{NS}
, we have to check that the pull-back of the morphism
{\mathcal{O}}\to{\mathcal{O}}(\Delta_{NS})
is zero.
Since the sources are smooth, it is enough to check this generically, so we can consider a neighborhood of the point corresponding to a supercurve with a single
NS node. Then we know that the local equation of
\Delta_{NS}
will be
(t=0)
, where
t
corresponds to the map to the universal deformation of the node.
Since the deformation of the node given by the source of the maps (
7.8
) and (
7.9
) is trivial, this proves that the pull-back of
t
with respect to one of these maps is zero. Using the fact that the morphism on tangent spaces is an embedding with the image which is the orthogonal to
dt
, we deduce
that near this point
\Delta_{NS}
is the schematic image of the morphism.
∎

7.6.
The Ramond node boundary components

Gluing along two Ramond punctures is more subtle.

First, we point out a certain “residual structure” we have on each Ramond puncture.
Let
R\subset X
be a Ramond puncture in a stable supercurve
X/S
.

Lemma 7.11
.

The map

\delta|_{R}:\Omega_{X/S}|_{R}\to\omega_{X/R}(R)|_{R}\simeq\omega_{R/S}

factors through the canonical projection
\Omega_{X/S}|_{R}\to\Omega_{R/S}
, and induces
an isomorphism

\overline{\delta}_{R}:\Omega_{R/S}\to\omega_{R/S}.

This induces a trivialization of
(\Omega_{R/S})^{\otimes 2}
(or equivalently, of
\omega_{R/S}^{\otimes 2}
)
which is locally given by
(d\theta)^{2}
, for
\theta
such that

{\mathcal{O}}_{R}={\mathcal{O}}_{S}[\theta]
,

Proof.

Recall that in appropriate étale local coordinates
(z,\theta)
,
R
is given by
(z=0)
and we have

\delta(dz)=z\theta[\frac{dz}{z}|d\theta],\ \ \delta(d\theta)=[\frac{dz}{z}|d\theta].

It follows that
\delta|_{R}(dz)=0
, and
the induced map
\overline{\delta}_{R}
is given by

\overline{\delta}_{R}(d\theta)=\overline{b},

where
\overline{b}
is the generator of
\omega_{R/S}
corresponding to
[\frac{dz}{z}|d\theta]
.

Since the relative dimension of
R
is
0|1
, we have in fact an isomorphism

\omega_{R/S}^{-1}\simeq\Omega_{R/S}
. Thus, we can view
\overline{\delta}_{R}
as a trivializing section of
(\Omega_{R/S})^{\otimes 2}
.
∎

The above Lemma implies that every Ramond puncture
R
has a preferred system of étale local relative coordinates
\theta_{i}
, such that over intersections one has

\theta_{j}=\pm\theta_{i}+a_{ij}
, where
a_{ij}
are functions on the base. Namely, we require that
(d\theta_{i})^{2}
is the canonical trivialization of
(\Omega_{R/S})^{\otimes 2}
.
In other words, we have a canonical principal bundle
{\mathcal{P}}_{R}\to S
with the structure supergroup
\Z/2\ltimes{\mathbb{A}}^{0|1}
.

We can restate the above structure in more invariant terms. Let
\pi:R\to S
denote the projection.
First, we observe that

\Phi_{R}:=\pi_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{R}/{\mathcal{O}}_{S}\simeq\operatorname{Ber}^{-1}\pi_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{R}

(7.10)

is a line bundle of rank
0|1
on
S
. Furthermore, we have a canonical isomorphism of odd line bundles on
R
,

\pi^{*}\Phi_{R}\rTo{\sim}\Omega_{R}

induced by the de Rham differential
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}/{\mathcal{O}}_{R}\to\Omega_{R}
. Furthermore, the trivialization of
(\Omega_{R})^{\otimes 2}

in Lemma
7.11
actually comes from the canonical trivialization of

\Phi_{R}^{2}
on
S
.

Let
\Theta\subset\operatorname{tot}(\Phi_{R})
be the
\Z/2
-torsor over
S
corresponding to
\Phi_{R}
.
Then the principal bundle
{\mathcal{P}}_{R}\to S
can be identified with the preimage of
\Theta
in
\operatorname{tot}(\pi_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{R})
under
the natural projection
p:\pi_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{R}\to\pi_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{R}/{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
.

Lemma 7.12
.

The group scheme
\operatorname{Aut}(\Phi_{R})\to S
can be identified with the group of automorphisms of
\pi_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{R}
, which are
identity on
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
, and induce
\pm\operatorname{id}
on
\pi_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{R}/{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
. This group scheme is an extension of
\Z/2
by
the line bundle
\Phi_{R}^{-1}
.

Now suppose
X/S
is a (possibly disconnected) stable supercurve with two R-punctures
R\subset X
and
R^{\prime}\subset X
,
equipped with an isomorphism
R\simeq R^{\prime}
over
S
.
Then we can glue
X
with itself along
R\simeq R^{\prime}
into a superscheme
Z
over
S
equipped with a finite morphism
j:X\to Z
.

Lemma 7.13
.

Assume that the isomorphism
\alpha:R\rTo{\sim}R^{\prime}
is such that
the induced isomorphism

\alpha^{*}\omega_{R^{\prime}/S}^{\otimes 2}\rTo{\sim}\omega_{R/S}^{\otimes 2}

is equal to
-1
, where we use the trivializations of
\omega_{R/S}^{\otimes 2}
and

\omega_{R^{\prime}/S}^{\otimes 2}
coming from the supercurve structure on
X
.
Then the glued superscheme
Z/S
has a natural stable supercurve structure, such that the derivation
\delta
is
uniquely determined from the commutative diagram

{diagram}

Proof.

To check that
\delta
is well defined, we need to check that the image of the composition

j_{*}\delta\circ r
belongs to
\omega_{Z/S}
.
For this we can argue locally. Let
(z,\theta)
be the standard local coordinates on
X
near the puncture
R
,
so that
R
is given by
(z=0)
, and

\delta(dz)=z\theta[\frac{dz}{z}|d\theta],\ \ \delta(d\theta)=[\frac{dz}{z}|d\theta].

Let also
(z^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})
be similar coordinates near
R^{\prime}
. Then
\alpha^{*}(d\theta^{\prime})^{2}=-d\theta
, so changing
\theta
to
\pm\theta+a
, we can assume
that
i\alpha^{*}(\theta^{\prime})=\theta
.
Let us set
\overline{\theta}=i\theta^{\prime}
. Then we have

\delta(dz^{\prime})=z^{\prime}\overline{\theta}[\frac{dz}{z}|d\overline{\theta}],\ \ \delta(d\overline{\theta})=-[\frac{dz^{\prime}}{z^{\prime}}|d\overline{\theta}].

Then we have relative coordinates on the glued scheme
Z
,
(z,z^{\prime},\theta)
, where
zz^{\prime}=0
,
\theta
restricts to
\theta
near
R
and to
\overline{\theta}
near
R^{\prime}
.
Since
\delta(z)
has no pole at
R
, we see that
(\delta(z),0)
belongs to
\omega_{Z/S}
. Similarly,
(0,\delta^{\prime}(z^{\prime}))
belongs to
\omega_{Z/S}
.
Finally,

(\delta(\theta),\delta^{\prime}(\overline{\theta}))=([\frac{dz}{z}|d\theta],-[\frac{dz^{\prime}}{z^{\prime}}|d\overline{\theta}]),

which comes from a section of
\omega_{Z/S}
.

Over the point, our construction can be recast as follows. We start with a Ramond spin curve
(C,p,p^{\prime},L)
, together with an identification

\alpha:L|_{p}\simeq L|_{p^{\prime}},

such that
\alpha^{2}=-1
(where we use trivializations of
\omega_{C}(p)|_{p}
and
\omega_{C}(p^{\prime})|_{p^{\prime}}
).
We glue
p
with
p^{\prime}
and get a nodal curve
Z_{0}
, and then descend
L
to a line bundle
{\mathcal{L}}
over
Z_{0}
using
\alpha
.
Then we have a natural isomorphism
{\mathcal{L}}^{\otimes 2}\simeq\omega_{Z_{0}}
, and one can easily check that the corresponding isomorphism

{\mathcal{L}}\rTo{\sim}\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}({\mathcal{L}},\omega_{Z_{0}})
is induced by
\delta^{-}
, where
\delta
is defined as above.
∎

The choice of an isomorphism
\alpha
can be interpreted in terms of the principal
\Z/2\ltimes{\mathbb{A}}^{0|1}
-bundles
{\mathcal{P}}_{R}
and
{\mathcal{P}}_{R^{\prime}}
as follows.
For every
c\in\C^{*}
let us denote by
[c]
the automorphism of
\Z/2\ltimes{\mathbb{A}}^{0|1}
given by the rescaling by
c
on
{\mathbb{A}}^{0|1}
(and trivial on
\Z/2
).
Then a choice of
\alpha
is equivalent to a choice of an isomorphism of
\Z/2\ltimes{\mathbb{A}}^{0|1}
-bundles,

{\mathcal{P}}_{R}\to[i]_{*}{\mathcal{P}}_{R^{\prime}},

where
[i]_{*}{\mathcal{P}}_{R^{\prime}}
is the push-out of
{\mathcal{P}}_{R^{\prime}}
with respect to the automorphism
[i]
.

We can apply the above construction to the two last Ramond punctures
R_{n+1}
and
R_{n+2}
on the universal stable supercurve over

\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}_{g;m,n+2}
.
Let
{\mathcal{P}}_{n+1}\to\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}_{g;m,n+2}
and
{\mathcal{P}}_{n+2}\to\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}_{g;m,n+2}
be the corresponding
principal
\Z/2\ltimes{\mathbb{A}}^{0|1}
-bundles.
Let
\operatorname{Isom}({\mathcal{P}}_{n+1},[i]_{*}{\mathcal{P}}_{n+2})\to\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}_{g;m,n+2}
denote the bundle of isomorphisms between these
\Z/2\ltimes{\mathbb{A}}^{0|1}
-bundles.
Note that it is also a principal bundle with the group
\Z/2\times{\mathbb{A}}^{0|1}
.

Then the above gluing construction gives a family of stable supercurves with a Ramond node over
\operatorname{Isom}({\mathcal{P}}_{n+1},[i]_{*}{\mathcal{P}}_{n+2})
, so we get a morphism

\operatorname{Isom}({\mathcal{P}}_{n+1},[i]_{*}{\mathcal{P}}_{n+2})\to\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}_{g+1;m,n}

(7.11)

Similarly, we can glue the last Ramond punctures
R,R^{\prime}
on the stable supercurves
X,X^{\prime}
over the moduli space

\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}_{g_{1};m_{1},n_{1}+1}\times\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}_{g_{2};m_{2},n_{2}+1}
pulled back from each factor.
This gives a morphism

\operatorname{Isom}({\mathcal{P}}_{R},[i]_{*}{\mathcal{P}}_{R^{\prime}})\to\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}_{g_{1}+g_{2};m_{1}+m_{2},n_{1}+n_{2}}.

(7.12)

Note that in this case
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
are odd, so
n_{1}+n_{2}\geq 2
.

Lemma 7.14
.

Both morphisms (
7.11
) and (
7.12
) induce embeddings of codimension
1|0
on tangent spaces
and factor through the divisor
\Delta_{R}
. Furthermore, near a stable supercurve with a single non-separating Ramond node the divisor
\Delta_{R}
coincides with the schematic image
of (
7.11
).

Proof.

Let
X
be the stable supercurve obtained by gluing two Ramond punctures
R,R^{\prime}
on a stable supercurve
\widetilde{X}
, via an isomorphism

\alpha:R\to R^{\prime}
as in Lemma
7.13
.
Note that we have an exact sequence

0\to\Pi\C\to T_{\operatorname{Def}(\widetilde{X},\alpha)}\to T_{\operatorname{Def}(\widetilde{X})}\to 0,

where the odd line
\Pi\C
corresponds to infinitesimal deformations of
\alpha
.
By Proposition
3.11
(i), we have

{\mathcal{A}}_{X}^{+}\simeq\rho_{*}{\mathcal{A}}_{\widetilde{X}}^{+}.

As in Lemma
7.10
we deduce from this that

T^{+}_{\operatorname{Def}(\widetilde{X},\alpha)}\to T^{+}_{\operatorname{Def}(X)}

is an embedding of codimension
1
.

Next, we claim that the map between the spaces of odd infinitesimal deformations,

T^{-}_{\operatorname{Def}(\widetilde{X},\alpha)}\to T^{-}_{\operatorname{Def}(X)}

is an isomorphism. Indeed, we know that every odd deformation of
X
is locally trivial, i.e.,

T^{-}_{\operatorname{Def}(X)}\simeq H^{1}(X,{\mathcal{A}}_{X}^{-}).

Also, by Proposition
3.11
(i),
we have an exact sequence

0\to\C\to H^{1}(X,{\mathcal{A}}_{X}^{-})\to H^{1}(\widetilde{X},{\mathcal{A}}_{\widetilde{X}}^{-})\to 0

so

\dim T^{-}_{\operatorname{Def}(X)}=\dim H^{1}(\widetilde{X},{\mathcal{A}}_{\widetilde{X}}^{-})+1=\dim T^{-}_{\operatorname{Def}(\widetilde{X},\alpha)}.

It remains to observe that every locally trivial deformation of
X

can be lifted to a deformation of
(\widetilde{X},\alpha)
.
∎

8.
Canonical line bundle on the moduli space of stable supercurves

In this section we will calculate the canonical line bundle of
\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}_{g,n_{NS},n_{R}}
, eventually proving Theorem B.

8.1.
What Kodaira-Spencer isomorphism gives for the canonical bundle

Set
\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}=\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}_{g,n_{NS},n_{R}}
. We are interested in the canonical line bundle
K_{\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}}=\operatorname{Ber}^{-1}({\mathcal{T}}_{\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}})
.
Over the smooth locus
{\mathcal{S}}\subset\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}
, from the Kodaira-Spencer isomorphism we get an isomorphism (see Sec.
6.4
)

\operatorname{ber}(KS_{\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}}):K_{{\mathcal{S}}}\rTo{\sim}\operatorname{Ber}^{-1}(R^{1}\pi_{*}{\mathcal{L}}(X,P_{\bullet},R_{\bullet}))\simeq\operatorname{Ber}(R\pi_{*}{\mathcal{L}}(X,P_{\bullet},R_{\bullet})),

(8.1)

where

{\mathcal{L}}(X,P_{\bullet},R_{\bullet}):=\omega_{X/S}^{-2}(-\sum_{i\in I}D_{i}-2\sum_{j\in J}R_{j}).

Recall that
here
\omega_{X/S}^{-2}
is the line bundle defined in Theorem
4.1
, and

D_{i}
are the divisors on the universal curve
X
associated with the NS punctures.

Our study in Sec.
6

of the behavior of the Kodaira-Spencer map in degenerating families of supercurves leads to the following identification of
K_{\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}}
.

Proposition 8.1
.

One has a natural isomorphism

\operatorname{ber}(KS_{\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}})^{-1}:K_{\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}}\rTo{\sim}\operatorname{Ber}(R\pi_{*}{\mathcal{L}}(X,P_{\bullet},R_{\bullet}))(-\Delta),

where
\Delta
is the boundary divisor.

Proof.

We just have to check that isomorphism (
8.1
) acquires simple poles at all generic points of
\Delta
.
But this follows from the results of Sec.
6
and our local description of
\Delta
at points corresponding to stable
supercurves with single nodes.
∎

Next, we will study the line bundle
\operatorname{Ber}(R\pi_{*}{\mathcal{L}}(X,P_{\bullet},R_{\bullet}))
. We begin with the case
when there are no punctures. Then the above identification of the canonical bundle becomes

\operatorname{ber}(KS_{\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}})^{-1}:K_{\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}}\rTo{\sim}\operatorname{Ber}(R\pi_{*}(\omega_{X/S}^{-2})).

8.2.
Isomorphisms for Berezinian bundles

Let
\pi:X\to S
be a smooth proper morphism of relative dimension
1|1
. Then we can define Deligne’s symbol

\langle L_{1}
,
L_{2}\rangle
of a pair of line bundles of rank
1|0

over
X
similarly to the even case (considered in
[
12
]
), so that for a relative effective Cartier divisor
D

one has

\langle{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D),L\rangle\simeq\operatorname{Ber}(\pi_{*}(L|_{D}))/\operatorname{Ber}(\pi_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{D}).

If
{\mathcal{B}}(L)
denotes the Berezinian of the derived push-forward of
L
then one has, as in the classical case,

{\mathcal{B}}(L_{1}\otimes L_{2})\otimes{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{O}})\simeq{\mathcal{B}}(L_{1})\otimes{\mathcal{B}}(L_{2})\otimes\langle L_{1},L_{2}\rangle.

However, in the supercase,
for any line bundles
L
and
L^{\prime}
of rank
1|0
,
there is a canonical isomorphism

\alpha_{D}:\operatorname{Ber}(\pi_{*}L|_{D})\rTo{\sim}\operatorname{Ber}(\pi_{*}(L^{\prime}|_{D})),

(8.2)

induced by any local isomorphism
L\to L^{\prime}
(the point is that the Berezinian of a scalar automorphism of a linear space of rank
1|1
is trivial). Hence, the Deligne’s symbol is canonically trivial, so we get

{\mathcal{B}}(L_{1}\otimes L_{2})\otimes{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{O}})\simeq{\mathcal{B}}(L_{1})\otimes{\mathcal{B}}(L_{2})

(8.3)

(see
[
32
]
).

More explicitly, locally over
S
we can pick a relative positive divisor
D
such that
L_{1}L_{2}(D)
,
L_{2}(D)
and
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(D)
have no
R^{1}\pi_{*}
,
and assume also that we have an even section
s\in H^{0}(X,L_{2}(D))
, fiberwise regular, vanishing on a relative divisor
E
.
Then we have the resolutions

\displaystyle R\pi_{*}({\mathcal{O}}_{X}):

\displaystyle[\pi_{*}L_{2}(D)\to\pi_{*}L_{2}(D)|_{E}],

\displaystyle R\pi_{*}(L_{1}):

\displaystyle[\pi_{*}L_{1}L_{2}(D)\to\pi_{*}L_{1}L_{2}(D)|_{E}],

\displaystyle R\pi_{*}(L_{2}):

\displaystyle[\pi_{*}L_{2}(D)\to\pi_{*}L_{2}(D)|_{D}],

\displaystyle R\pi_{*}(L_{1}L_{2}):

\displaystyle[\pi_{*}L_{1}L_{2}(D)\to\pi_{*}L_{1}L_{2}(D)|_{D}].

Using these resolutions we get an isomorphism

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}(L_{1}L_{2})\otimes{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})={\mathcal{B}}(L_{1}L_{2}(D))\otimes{\mathcal{B}}(L_{2}(D))\otimes\operatorname{Ber}^{-1}(\pi_{*}L_{1}L_{2}(D)|_{D})\otimes\operatorname{Ber}^{-1}(\pi_{*}L_{2}(D)|_{E})

\displaystyle\rTo{\operatorname{id}\otimes\operatorname{id}\otimes\alpha^{-1}_{D}\otimes\alpha^{-1}_{E}}{\mathcal{B}}(L_{1}L_{2}(D))\otimes{\mathcal{B}}(L_{2}(D))\otimes\operatorname{Ber}^{-1}(\pi_{*}L_{2}(D)|_{D})\otimes\operatorname{Ber}^{-1}(\pi_{*}L_{1}L_{2}(D)|_{E})={\mathcal{B}}(L_{1})\otimes{\mathcal{B}}(L_{2}).

Note that isomorphism (
8.3
) can be used to calculate
{\mathcal{B}}(L_{1}L_{2})
when
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
are not necessarily of
rank
1|0
: for a line bundle
L
of rank
0|1
we use the isomorphism

{\mathcal{B}}(\Pi L)\simeq{\mathcal{B}}(L)^{-1}.

For example, for a line bundle
L
of rank
1|0
one has

{\mathcal{B}}(L^{3})\simeq{\mathcal{B}}(L)^{3}/{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{O}})^{2}.

Applying this to
L=\Pi\omega_{X}
, where
X/S
is a smooth supercurve,
one gets the analog of Mumford isomorphism in the supercase (see
[
32
]
),

{\mathcal{B}}(\omega_{X/S}^{3})\simeq{\mathcal{B}}(\omega_{X/S})^{3}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})^{2}\simeq{\mathcal{B}}(\omega_{X/S})^{5},

(8.4)

since
{\mathcal{B}}(L)\simeq{\mathcal{B}}(\omega_{X/S}\otimes L^{-1})
by Grothendieck-Serre duality.
Thus, denoting
\operatorname{Ber}_{i}:={\mathcal{B}}(\omega_{X/S}^{i})
, we can write this isomorphism as

\operatorname{Ber}_{3}\simeq\operatorname{Ber}_{1}^{5}.

We will need an extension of isomorphism (
8.3
) to families of stable supercurves
\pi:X\to S
and to the case when
L_{1}
is replaced by a not necessarily
locally free sheaf. For a coherent sheaf
{\mathcal{F}}
over
X
, flat over
S
, we denote by
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{F}})
the Berezinian of the perfect complex
R\pi_{*}({\mathcal{F}})
.

Lemma 8.2
.

Let
\pi:X\to S
be a stable supercurve,
{\mathcal{F}}
a coherent sheaf on
X
flat over
S
.
Assume that
{\mathcal{F}}
is locally free of rank
1|0
over the smooth locus of
\pi
. Then for any line bundle
L
of rank
1|0
on
X
, one has
a canonical isomorphism

{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{F}}\otimes L)\simeq{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{F}})\otimes{\mathcal{B}}(L)\otimes{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})^{-1}.

(8.5)

Proof.

We can use the similar recipe as above to construct this isomorphism locally over
S
: we simply replace
L_{1}
by
{\mathcal{F}}
,
L_{2}
by
L
and
take
D
with support in the smooth locus of
\pi
, and a section
s\in H^{0}(X,L(D))
with the vanishing divisor
E
, also contained in the smooth locus of
\pi
.
∎

8.3.
Behavior of the super Mumford isomorphism near the NS boundary divisor

Now we want to look at the behavior of the super Mumford isomorphism (
8.4
)
near the generic point of boundary divisors. In the case of a Ramond node it extends to an isomorphism, since
\omega_{X/S}
is
still a line bundle. Thus, it remains to study locally the boundary component where one NS node appears. So let

\pi:X\to S
be a family of stable supercurves as in Sec.
6.2
, so we have a smooth morphism
t:S\to{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
, a relative NS node
q:S_{0}\to X_{0}\subset X
over
the divisor
S_{0}=(t=0)\subset S
, and
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
has the standard description along
q(S_{0})
.

Let
S^{\prime}=S\setminus S_{0}
, and let
X^{\prime}=\pi^{-1}(S^{\prime})
be the corresponding family of smooth supercurves.
First, we need to explain how to extend the line bundles
{\mathcal{B}}(\omega_{X^{\prime}/S^{\prime}}^{3})
and
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{O}}_{X^{\prime}})
to
S
.
For the second one this is straightforward: the extension is given by
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})
.

For any integer
n
let us set

\omega_{X/S}^{n}:=j_{*}\omega_{U/S}^{n},

where
U\subset X
is the smooth locus of
\pi
.
Recall that
\omega_{X/S}^{2}
is a line bundle on
X
(see Theorem
4.1
). Thus, we have

\omega_{X/S}^{3}=j_{*}\omega_{U/S}^{3}\simeq\omega_{X/S}^{2}\otimes j_{*}\omega_{U/S}\simeq\omega_{X/S}^{2}\otimes\omega_{X/S}.

This is a coherent sheaf, flat over
S
, so we can take
{\mathcal{B}}(\omega_{X/S}^{3})
as the desired extension of

{\mathcal{B}}(\omega_{X^{\prime}/S^{\prime}}^{3})
.

Note that by Grothendieck-Serre duality, we have an isomorphism of line bundes on
S
,

{\mathcal{B}}(\omega_{X/S})\simeq{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{O}}_{X}).

Since
\omega_{X/S}
is a coherent sheaf flat over
S
, we deduce from Lemma
8.2
an isomorphism

{\mathcal{B}}(\Pi\omega_{X/S}^{3})\simeq{\mathcal{B}}(\Pi\omega_{X/S})\otimes{\mathcal{B}}(\omega_{X/S}^{2})\otimes{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})^{-1}\simeq{\mathcal{B}}(\omega_{X/S}^{2})\otimes{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})^{-2},

or equivalently,

{\mathcal{B}}(\omega_{X/S}^{3})\simeq{\mathcal{B}}(\omega_{X/S}^{2})^{-1}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})^{2}.

(8.6)

Thus, our problem reduces to studying the behavior of the isomorphism on
X^{\prime}
,

{\mathcal{B}}(\omega_{X^{\prime}/S^{\prime}}^{2})\simeq{\mathcal{B}}(\Pi\omega_{X^{\prime}/S^{\prime}})^{2}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{O}}_{X^{\prime}})^{-1}\simeq{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{O}}_{X^{\prime}})^{-3}

near the divisor
S_{0}\subset S
. To this end we look more carefully at the recipe for this isomorphism outlined in Sec.
8.2
.

We can pick a sufficiently positive relative divisor
D
supported in the smooth locus of
\pi
, and also an even section
s
of
\Pi\omega_{X/S}(D)

with the zero locus
E
. Furthermore, we want to make a special choice of
s
as described below.

Lemma 8.3
.

There exists an exact sequence of
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-modules,

0\to\omega^{\operatorname{reg}}_{X/S}\to\omega_{X/S}\to q_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{S_{0}}\to 0

such that in the formal neighborhood of
q(S_{0})
,
\omega^{\operatorname{reg}}_{X/S}
is the
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-submodule generated by the sections
s_{1}
and
s_{2}

given by (
6.12
).
Furthermore, we can choose an even section
s
of
\Pi\omega^{\operatorname{reg}}_{X/S}(D)
in such a way that in the formal neighborhood of
q
one has

s=v\cdot(s_{1}+u\cdot s_{2}),

for some invertible functions
u
and
v
that are congruent to
1
modulo the ideal of
q(S_{0})
.
By making a change of variables

z_{1}\mapsto u^{-1}z_{1},\ \ z_{2}\mapsto uz_{2},\ \ \theta_{1}\mapsto\theta_{1},\ \ \theta_{2}\mapsto u\theta_{2}

we can achieve that
u=1
.

Proof.

In the formal neighborhood of
q
, the quotient of
\omega_{X/S}/({\mathcal{O}}_{X}s_{1}+{\mathcal{O}}_{X}s_{2})
is generated by
s_{0}
and is isomorphic to
q_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{S_{0}}
.
This implies the first assertion. Furthermore, using Lemma
6.4
, we see that

\omega^{\operatorname{reg}}_{X/S}\otimes q_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{S_{0}}
is a free
q_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{S_{0}}
-module generated by the images of
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
.
For sufficiently positive
D
, the map

H^{0}(X,\omega^{\operatorname{reg}}_{X/S}(D))\to H^{0}(X,\omega^{\operatorname{reg}}_{X/S}\otimes q_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{S_{0}})

is surjective, so we can choose an odd section
s\in H^{0}(X,\omega^{\operatorname{reg}}_{X/S}(D))
such that

s\equiv s_{1}+s_{2}\mod J_{q(S_{0})}.

Thus, in the formal neighborhood of
q
, we can write
s=u_{1}s_{1}+u_{2}s_{2}
where
u_{i}\equiv 1\mod J_{q(S_{0})}
.

It is easy to check that the given change of variables transforms
s
in the desired way.
∎

Now we define coherent sheaves
{\mathcal{F}}
and
{\mathcal{G}}
on
X
by the exact sequences

0\to{\mathcal{O}}_{X}\rTo{s}\Pi\omega_{X/S}(D)\to{\mathcal{F}}\to 0,

0\to\Pi\omega_{X/S}\rTo{\cdot s}\omega_{X/S}^{2}(D)\to{\mathcal{G}}\to 0.

Let
\hat{{\mathcal{F}}}
and
\hat{{\mathcal{G}}}
denote
the completions of
{\mathcal{F}}
and
{\mathcal{G}}
at
q
.

Lemma 8.4
.

(a) The space
\hat{{\mathcal{F}}}
is a free
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
-module with the basis
s_{1},s_{0}
.

(b) We have an isomorphism
\hat{{\mathcal{G}}}\simeq{\mathcal{O}}_{Z}
, where
Z\subset X
is a sub-superscheme given by

z_{1}=-t,\ \ z_{2}=t,\ \ \theta_{1}+\theta_{2}=0.

In particular,
\hat{{\mathcal{G}}}
is a free
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
-module with the basis
e,\theta_{1}e
,
where
e
is the local generator of
\omega_{X/S}^{2}
given by (
6.13
).
The sheaf
\hat{{\mathcal{F}}}
is also scheme-theoretically supported on
Z
.

(c) The Berezinian of the map of
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
-modules

s_{1}\cdot?:\hat{{\mathcal{F}}}\to\hat{{\mathcal{G}}}

is equal to
ft
, where
f
is an invertible function.

Proof.

(a) Lemma
6.4
immediately shows that the map

{\mathcal{O}}_{S}\to\Pi\omega_{X/S}:f\mapsto f\cdot(s_{1}+s_{2})

is injective.
Furthermore, the submodule
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}(s_{1}+s_{2})
has the following topological basis over
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
:

\displaystyle s_{1}+s_{2},\ \ z_{1}^{n+1}(s_{1}+s_{2})=z_{1}^{n}(z_{1}+t)s_{1},\ \ z_{2}^{n}(z_{2}-t)s_{2},\ \ \theta_{1}(s_{1}+s_{2})=\theta_{1}s_{1}+ts_{0},

\displaystyle z_{1}^{n+1}\theta_{1}(s_{1}+s_{2})=z_{1}^{n}(z_{1}+t)\theta_{1}s_{1},\ \ \theta_{2}(s_{1}+s_{2})=\theta_{2}s_{2}+ts_{0},\ \ z_{2}^{n}(z_{2}-t)\theta_{2}s_{2},

where
n\geq 0
.
Hence, the quotient by this submodule has the images of
s_{0},s_{1}
as a basis over
{\mathcal{O}}_{S}
.

(b) Near the node,
\omega_{X/S}^{2}
is a free
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-module with one even generator
e
. Furthermore, it
is easy to check that with respect to the map
\omega_{X/S}^{\otimes 2}\to\omega_{X/S}^{2}
one has

s_{1}^{2}=z_{1}e,\ \ s_{2}^{2}=-z_{2}e,\ \ s_{1}s_{2}=te,\ \ s_{0}s_{i}=\theta_{i}e,

for
i=1,2
.
Thus, the image of the multiplication by
s_{1}+s_{2}
is the
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-submodule generated
by

s_{1}(s_{1}+s_{2})=(z_{1}+t)e,\ \ s_{2}(s_{1}+s_{2})=(t-z_{2})e,\ \ s_{0}(s_{1}+s_{2})=(\theta_{1}+\theta_{2})e.

In other words, we have an identification

\hat{{\mathcal{G}}}\simeq{\mathcal{O}}_{X}/(z_{1}+t,z_{2}-t,\theta_{1}+\theta_{2})={\mathcal{O}}_{Z}\simeq{\mathcal{O}}_{S}[\theta_{1}],

as claimed.

(c) The operator of multiplication by
s_{1}
acts on the bases of
\hat{{\mathcal{F}}}
and
\hat{{\mathcal{G}}}
as

s_{1}\mapsto s_{1}^{2}=-te,\ \ s_{0}\mapsto s_{1}s_{0}=\theta_{1}e,

Hence, the Berezinian is equal to
-t
(recall that
s_{1}
is an even generator of
\hat{{\mathcal{F}}}
, while
s_{0}
is an odd generator).
∎

Let us consider the following resolutions for the derived push-forwards under
\pi
:

\displaystyle R\pi_{*}({\mathcal{O}}_{X}):

\displaystyle[\pi_{*}(\Pi\omega_{X/S}(D)\to\pi_{*}{\mathcal{F}}],

\displaystyle R\pi_{*}(\Pi\omega_{X/S}):

\displaystyle[\pi_{*}(\omega_{X/S}^{2}(D))\to\pi_{*}{\mathcal{G}}],

\displaystyle R\pi_{*}(\Pi\omega_{X/S}):

\displaystyle[\pi_{*}(\Pi\omega_{X/S}(D))\to\pi_{*}(\Pi\omega_{X/S}(D)|_{D})],

\displaystyle R\pi_{*}(\omega_{X/S}^{2}):

\displaystyle[\pi_{*}(\omega_{X/S}^{2}(D))\to\pi_{*}(\omega_{X/S}^{2}(D)|_{D})].

These resolutions give a canonical isomorphism over
S
,

{\mathcal{B}}(\omega_{X/S}^{2})\otimes{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})\simeq\operatorname{Ber}(R\pi_{*}(\Pi\omega_{X/S}))^{\otimes 2}\otimes\operatorname{Ber}(\pi_{*}{\mathcal{G}})\otimes\operatorname{Ber}(\pi_{*}{\mathcal{F}})^{-1}.

(8.7)

Now the super Mumford isomorphism for the induced smooth family

X_{S^{\prime}}\to S^{\prime}
is obtained by choosing an isomorphism of

{\mathcal{F}}|_{X_{S^{\prime}}}\simeq{\mathcal{G}}|_{X_{S^{\prime}}}

(where both sheaves are supported on the zero divisor of
s
in
X_{S^{\prime}}
)
and using the Berezinian of the induced map on
R\pi_{*}
to get an isomorphism of
\operatorname{Ber}(\pi_{*}{\mathcal{F}})|_{S^{\prime}}
with
\operatorname{Ber}(\pi_{*}{\mathcal{G}})|_{S^{\prime}}
(see (
8.2
)).
For this we are going to construct a morphism

\alpha:{\mathcal{F}}\to{\mathcal{G}},

which is an isomorphism away from the node, and use its restriction to
X_{S^{\prime}}
.

Note that both
{\mathcal{F}}
and
{\mathcal{G}}
are supported on
Z\sqcup E
,
where
E
is a relative divisor supported in the smooth locus of
\pi
, so
we have decompositions into subsheaves supported on
Z
and on
E
,

{\mathcal{F}}\simeq{\mathcal{F}}_{Z}\oplus{\mathcal{F}}_{E},\ \ {\mathcal{G}}\simeq{\mathcal{G}}_{Z}\oplus{\mathcal{G}}_{E}.

We can choose separately morphisms
{\mathcal{F}}_{E}\to{\mathcal{G}}_{E}
and
{\mathcal{F}}_{Z}\to{\mathcal{G}}_{Z}
.

We have

{\mathcal{F}}_{E}\simeq\pi_{*}(\Pi\omega_{X/S}(D)|_{E}),\ \ {\mathcal{G}}_{E}\simeq\pi_{*}(\omega_{X/S}^{2}(D)|_{E}),

so both are isomorphic to
{\mathcal{O}}_{E}
, and we choose any isomorphism between them as a morphism
\alpha_{E}:{\mathcal{F}}_{E}\to{\mathcal{G}}_{E}
.
On the other hand, we have

{\mathcal{F}}_{Z}\simeq\hat{F},\ \ {\mathcal{G}}_{Z}\simeq\hat{{\mathcal{G}}},

Hence, we can use the morphism of
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
-modules,

\mu_{s_{1}}:\hat{{\mathcal{F}}}\to\hat{{\mathcal{G}}},

given by the multiplication with
s_{1}
, as our morphism
{\mathcal{F}}_{Z}\to{\mathcal{G}}_{Z}
.
By Lemma
8.4
, it restricts to an isomorphism over
t\neq 0
.
Thus, we get the desired morphism

\alpha=(\mu_{s^{\prime}},\alpha_{E}):{\mathcal{F}}\to{\mathcal{G}}.

By definition, the super Mumford isomorphism for
X_{S^{\prime}}\to S^{\prime}
is obtained by restricting the isomorphism of ilne bundles
(
8.7
) to
S^{\prime}
and multiplying it with
\operatorname{ber}R\pi_{*}(\alpha|_{X_{S^{\prime}}})^{-1}\in{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{F}})\otimes{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{G}})^{-1}
.
Since
\operatorname{ber}R\pi_{*}(\alpha_{E^{\prime}})
is invertible on
S
, we see that

\operatorname{ber}R\pi_{*}(\alpha|_{X_{S^{\prime}}})=u\cdot\operatorname{ber}\pi_{*}(\mu_{s_{1}}),

where
u
is an invertible function on
S
.
Using Lemma
8.4
(iii), we deduce that

\operatorname{ber}R\pi_{*}(\alpha|_{X_{S^{\prime}}})^{-1}=u^{\prime}\cdot t^{-1},

for some invertible function
u^{\prime}
on
S
, with respect to some
bases of
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{F}})
and
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{G}})
on
S
.

Thus, we obtained an isomorphism

{\mathcal{B}}(\omega_{X/S}^{2})\rTo{\sim}{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})^{-1}\otimes\operatorname{Ber}(R\pi_{*}(\Pi\omega_{X/S}))^{\otimes 2}(\Delta),

(8.8)

where
\Delta
is the divisor given by
t=0
. Combining this with isomorphism
(
8.6
), we get the following local result.

Proposition 8.5
.

In the above situation the super Mumford isomorphism

{\mathcal{B}}(\omega_{X^{\prime}/S^{\prime}}^{-2})\simeq{\mathcal{B}}(\omega_{X^{\prime}/S^{\prime}}^{3})\simeq{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{O}}_{X^{\prime}})^{5}

extends to an isomorphism

{\mathcal{B}}(\omega_{X/S}^{-2})\simeq{\mathcal{B}}(\omega_{X/S}^{3})\simeq{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})^{5}(-\Delta).

Combining this with Corollary
6.7
and with Proposition
6.9
we obtain the following result.

Theorem 8.6
.

Let
\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}_{g}
denote the moduli stack of stable supercurves of genus
g
, and
let
\Delta_{NS}\subset\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}_{g}
(resp.,
\Delta_{R}\subset\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}_{g}
) denote the boundary divisor supported on the locus where a supercurve
acquires an NS node (resp., a Ramond node), defined in Sec.
7.2
.
Then one has an isomorphism of line bundles

K_{\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}_{g}}\simeq\operatorname{Ber}_{1}^{5}(-2\Delta_{NS}-\Delta_{R}).

(8.9)

Proof.

Let
\pi:X\to\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}_{g}
denote the universal supercurve.
First, we have an isomorphism

K_{\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}_{g}}\simeq\operatorname{Ber}^{-1}R^{1}\pi_{*}\omega_{X/\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}_{g}}^{-2}(-\Delta_{NS}-\Delta_{R})\simeq{\mathcal{B}}(\omega_{X/\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}_{g}}^{-2})(-\Delta_{NS}-\Delta_{R})

(see Corollary
6.7
and Proposition
6.9
).
Next, by Proposition
8.5
, we have

{\mathcal{B}}(\omega_{X/\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}_{g}}^{-2})\simeq{\mathcal{B}}(\omega_{X/\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}_{g}}^{3})\simeq\operatorname{Ber}_{1}^{5}(-\Delta_{NS})

(recall that the super-Mumford isomorphism (
8.4
) extends over the Ramond type boundary divisor
\Delta_{R}
).
Combining this with the previous isomorphism we get the result.
∎

8.4.
Canonical line bundle on the moduli space of stable supercurves with punctures

Let
\pi:X\to S
be a stable supercurve with NS punctures
P_{1},\ldots,P_{m}
and Ramond divisors
R_{1},\ldots,R_{n}
.
We denote by

D_{i}\subset X
the divisors associated with
P_{i}
as in Sec.
2.5
.

For every
i=1,\ldots,m
, let us define a line bundle of rank
0|1
on
S
,

\Psi_{i}:=P_{i}^{*}\omega_{X/S}.

Lemma 8.7
.

One has natural isomorphisms of line bundles of rank
0|1
on
S
,

\Psi_{i}\simeq\pi_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{D_{i}}/{\mathcal{O}}_{S},

\Psi_{i}^{-1}\simeq P_{i}^{*}\omega_{X/S}(D_{i})\simeq P_{i}^{*}\omega_{D_{i}/S}\simeq\operatorname{Ber}(\pi_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{D_{i}}).

Hence, for any line bundle
L
of rank
1|0
on
X
, one has

\operatorname{Ber}(\pi_{*}L|_{D_{i}})\simeq\Psi_{i}^{-1},

while for a line bundle
M
of rank
0|1
, one has

\operatorname{Ber}(\pi_{*}M|_{D_{i}})\simeq\Psi_{i}.

Proof.

Let us set
\Psi=\Psi_{i}
and

\widetilde{\Psi}:=\pi_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{D}/{\mathcal{O}}_{S},

where
D=D_{i}
and
P=P_{i}
.
Then
\widetilde{\Psi}
is a line bundle of rank
0|1
, and we have

\operatorname{Ber}(\pi_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{D})\simeq\widetilde{\Psi}^{-1}.

Hence, from (
8.2
) we get the last two isomorphisms with
\Psi_{i}
replaced by
\widetilde{\Psi}
.

Applying this to the line bundle
\omega_{X/S}^{-2}
of rank
1|0
, we get an isomorphism

\operatorname{Ber}(\pi_{*}\omega_{X/S}^{-2}|_{D})\simeq\widetilde{\Psi}^{-1}.

But from Lemma
2.14
, we have an isomorphism over
S
,

\pi_{*}(\omega_{X/S}^{-2}|_{D})\simeq{\mathcal{T}}_{X/S}|_{P}.

Hence, passing to the Berezinians we get

\widetilde{\Psi}^{-1}\simeq\omega_{X/S}^{-1}|_{P},

i.e.,
\widetilde{\Psi}\simeq\Psi
.

Next, let
I_{D}\subset I_{P}\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
be the ideal sheaves of
D=D_{i}
and
P=P_{i}
. Since the projection to
S
induces an isomorphism
P\simeq S
,
we have a decomposition

\pi_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{D}={\mathcal{O}}_{S}\oplus\pi_{*}(I_{P}/I_{D}),

so

\Psi\simeq\widetilde{\Psi}\simeq\pi_{*}(I_{P}/I_{D}).

Note also that
I_{P}^{2}\subset I_{D}
, so
I_{P}/I_{D}
can be identified with the conormal sheaf to
P
in
D
.
Now, since
\omega_{P/S}={\mathcal{O}}_{S}
, the
exact sequence

0\to\Psi\to\Omega_{D/S}|_{P}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{P}\to 0

gives an isomorphism

P^{*}\omega_{D/S}\simeq\operatorname{Ber}(\Psi)=\Psi^{-1}.

∎

Corollary 8.8
.

The line bundle
P_{i}^{*}\omega_{X/S}^{2}(D_{i})
on
S
is canonically trivialized.

Example 8.9
.

Suppose
X\to S
,
(P_{i})
, is a family of supercurves with NS-punctures over an
even
base
S
.
Then
{\mathcal{O}}_{X}={\mathcal{O}}_{C}\oplus L
, where
(C,L)
is the underlying family of curves with spin-structures, and

{\mathcal{I}}_{P_{i}}={\mathcal{I}}_{p_{i}}\oplus L
, where
p_{i}\subset C
are marked points on
C
. We also have
{\mathcal{I}}_{D}={\mathcal{I}}_{p_{i}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{C}}{\mathcal{O}}_{X}
.
Thus,

\Psi_{i}^{-1}=P_{i}^{*}(\omega_{X/S}(D_{i}))=P_{i}^{*}(L(p_{i})\oplus\omega_{C/S}(p_{i}))\simeq p_{i}^{*}L(p_{i})\simeq p_{i}^{*}L^{-1},

where the last isomorphisms is induced by the trivialization of
L^{2}(p_{i})|_{p_{i}}\simeq\omega_{C/S}(p_{i})|_{p_{i}}
.

Remark 8.10
.

Note that for an NS-puncture
P_{i}
and the corresponding divisor
D_{i}
, the decomposition
(
2.11
) can be rewritten as

\pi_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{D_{i}}={\mathcal{O}}_{S}\oplus\Psi_{i},

where
\Psi_{i}
is a square-zero ideal.
For any line bundle
L
over
D_{i}
we have an exact sequence

0\to P_{i_{*}}(\Psi_{i}\otimes P_{i}^{*}L)\to L\to P_{i*}P_{i}^{*}L\to 0

In the case
L=\omega_{X/S}(D_{i})|_{D_{i}}=\omega_{D_{i}/S}
, the induced exact sequence of push-forwards to
S
has a splitting

\pi_{*}(\omega_{X/S}(D_{i})/\omega_{X/S})\to{\mathcal{O}}_{S}

given by the residue map for the
0|1
-dimensional superscheme
D_{i}/S
, so we have a decomposition

\pi_{*}(\omega_{D_{i}/S})=\Psi_{i}^{-1}\oplus{\mathcal{O}}_{S}.

For every Ramond divisor
R_{j}\subset X
let us consider the line bundle on the base,

\Phi_{j}:=\operatorname{Ber}(\pi_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{R_{j}})^{-1}\simeq\operatorname{Ber}(\pi_{*}\omega_{R_{j}}).

Note that
\Phi_{j}=\Phi_{R_{j}}
(see (
7.10
)), so the line bundles
\Phi_{j}^{2}
are canonically trivialized.

Let us consider the line bundle of rank
1|0
over
X
,

{\mathcal{L}}(X,P_{\bullet},R_{\bullet}):=\omega_{X/S}^{-2}(-\sum_{i}D_{i}-2\sum_{j}R_{j}).

Lemma 8.11
.

One has a natural isomorphism

{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{L}}(X,P_{\bullet},R_{\bullet}))\simeq{\mathcal{B}}(\omega_{X/S}^{3})\otimes\bigotimes_{i}\Psi_{i}

Proof.

Let us set
D=\sum_{i}D_{i}
,
R=\sum_{j}R_{j}
.
First, the Grothendieck duality gives an isomorphism

{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{L}}(X,P_{\bullet},R_{\bullet}))\simeq{\mathcal{B}}(\omega_{X/S}^{3}(D+2R)).

Now using the exact sequence

0\to\omega_{X/S}^{3}(2R)\to\omega_{X/S}^{3}(2R)(D)\to\omega_{X/S}^{3}(2R)(D)|_{D}\to 0,

(8.10)

we get using (
8.2
) and Lemma
8.7
,

{\mathcal{B}}(\omega_{X/S}^{3}(D+2R))\simeq{\mathcal{B}}(\omega_{X/S}^{3}(2R))\otimes\operatorname{Ber}(\pi_{*}\omega_{X/{\mathcal{M}}}^{3}(D)|_{D})\simeq{\mathcal{B}}(\omega_{X/M}^{3}(2R))\otimes\bigotimes_{i}\Psi_{i}.

Similarly, using the fitration of
\omega_{X/S}^{3}(2R)/\omega_{X/S}^{3}
with subquotients

\omega_{X/S}^{3}(2R)|_{R}
and
\omega_{X/S}^{3}(R)|_{R}
, we get an isomorphism

{\mathcal{B}}(\omega_{X/S}^{3}(2R))\simeq{\mathcal{B}}(\omega_{X/S}^{3})\otimes\bigotimes_{j}\Phi_{j}^{2}.

It remains to we use the trivializations of
\Phi_{j}^{2}
.
∎

Now the same argument as in Theorem
8.6
gives the formula for the canonical line bundle on the moduli stack of stable supercurves with punctures.

Proof of Theorem B.

Let
\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}=\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}_{g,n_{NS},n_{R}}
. We combine the isomorphism

K_{\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}}\simeq\operatorname{Ber}^{-1}R^{1}\pi_{*}{\mathcal{L}}(X,P_{\bullet},R_{\bullet})(-\Delta_{NS}-\Delta_{R})\simeq{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{L}}(X,P_{\bullet},R_{\bullet}))(-\Delta_{NS}-\Delta_{R})

(see Sec.
6.4
) with Lemma
8.11
and the isomophism

{\mathcal{B}}(\omega_{X/\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}}^{3})\simeq\operatorname{Ber}_{1}^{5}(-\Delta_{NS}).

This gives the required isomorphism

K_{\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}}\simeq\operatorname{Ber}_{1}^{5}\otimes\bigotimes_{i=1}^{m}\Psi_{i}(-2\Delta_{NS}-\Delta_{R}),

(8.11)

∎

9.
Splitting at the boundary divisor

Now we are going to study the restriction of the isomorphism of Theorem B to the boundary divisor. Using our presentation of the line bundle corresponding
to the boundary divisor as a Berezinian (see Sec.
7.2
) we find a natural identification of the normal line bundle to the boundary divisor.
Then we give a proof of Theorem C concerning the NS boundary component. We also give a conjectural statement for the Ramond boundary component.

9.1.
NS boundary components

Let
\iota:B\to\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}
be the standard gluing map covering an NS type boundary component, i.e., one of the maps (
7.8
) or
(
7.9
), restricted to the locus of smooth supercurves.
Let
X_{B}\to B
denote the universal stable supercurve, which is obtained by identifying two NS punctures
P_{1},P_{2}
on a smooth supercurve

\widetilde{X}\to B
into a node
Q\subset X_{B}
. Let
D_{1},D_{2}\subset\widetilde{X}
be the corresponding divisors. Note that we have a finite morphism
\rho:\widetilde{X}\to X_{B}
, and an exact sequence
on
X_{B}
,

0\to{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{B}}\to\rho_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{X}}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{Q}\to 0.

In particular, the Berezinian line bundle
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{O}}_{X_{B}})
for the family
X_{B}\to B
is naturally identified with
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{X}})
defined for the family
\widetilde{X}\to B
.

Note that in the case of a separating node, where
B={\mathcal{S}}_{1}\times{\mathcal{S}}_{2}
, the line bundle
{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{X}})
is the exterior product of two similar line bundles on the factors

{\mathcal{S}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{S}}_{2}
.

We can rewrite (
8.11
) near
B
as an isomorphism

K_{\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}}(2\Delta_{NS})\simeq{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})^{5}.

Thus, pulling it back to
B
leads to an isomorphism

K_{B}\otimes N_{B}\simeq{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{O}}_{X_{B}})^{5},

where
N_{B}
is the normal bundle defined as the pull-back of
{\mathcal{O}}(\Delta_{NS})
to
B
.
Note that the universal supercurve over
B
is equipped with two NS punctures
P_{1},P_{2}
and the isomorphism (
8.11
)
in smaller genus gives

K_{B}\simeq{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{X}})^{5}\otimes\Psi_{1}\otimes\Psi_{2}.

Comparing with the previous isomorphism we get an isomorphism

N_{B}\simeq\Psi_{1}^{-1}\otimes\Psi_{2}^{-1}.

Below we will define such a canonical isomorphism independently and then will check its compatibility with two above isomorphisms.

First, recall that we have a line bundle
\omega_{X/S}^{2}
defined on the universal curve of
\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}
by extending from the smooth locus (see Theorem
4.1
).

Lemma 9.1
.

(i) For any integer
m
let us set

\omega_{X_{B}/B}^{2m}:=\omega_{X/\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}}^{2m}|_{B}.

Then one has an exact sequence on
X_{B}
,

0\to\omega_{X_{B}/B}^{2m}\to\rho_{*}\omega_{\widetilde{X}/B}^{2m}(mD_{1}+mD_{2})\to{\mathcal{O}}_{Q}\to 0

(9.1)

where
Q\subset X_{B}
is the relative node obtained by gluing
P_{1}\simeq B\simeq P_{2}
.
Here we use the canonical trivializations of the restrictions of
\omega_{\widetilde{X}/B}^{2}(D_{1}+D_{2})
to
P_{1}
and
P_{2}

(see Corollary
8.8
).
Hence, we have a natural isomorphism

{\mathcal{B}}(\omega_{X/\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}}^{2m})|_{B}\simeq{\mathcal{B}}(\omega_{\widetilde{X}/B}^{2m}(mD_{1}+mD_{2})).

(9.2)

(ii) Let us set
\omega_{X_{B}/B}^{2m+1}:=\omega_{X_{B}/B}^{2m}\otimes\omega_{X_{B}/B}
. Then one has an exact sequence on
X_{B}
,

0\to\rho_{*}\omega^{2m+1}_{\widetilde{X}/B}(mD_{1}+mD_{2})\to\omega_{X_{B}/B}^{2m+1}\to Q_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{S}\to 0.

(9.3)

Hence, we have a natural isomorphism

{\mathcal{B}}(\omega_{X/\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}}^{2m+1})|_{B}\simeq{\mathcal{B}}(\omega_{\widetilde{X}/B}^{2m+1}(mD_{1}+mD_{2})).

(9.4)

In addition, we have a natural exact sequence

0\to\omega_{X_{B}/B}^{2m+1}\to\rho_{*}\omega_{\widetilde{X}/B}^{2m+1}((m+1)D_{1}+(m+1)D_{2})\to{\mathcal{C}}\to 0,

where
{\mathcal{C}}
is a sheaf supported on the node fitting into an exact sequence

0\to Q_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{S}\to{\mathcal{C}}\to Q_{*}(\Psi_{1}^{-1}\oplus\Psi_{2}^{-1})\to 0.

(9.5)

(iii) One has an exact sequence on
X_{B}
,

0\to{\mathcal{K}}\to\Omega_{X_{B}/B}\to\rho_{*}\Omega_{\widetilde{X}/B}\to 0,

where the sheaf
{\mathcal{K}}
is supported on the node, and has a filtration with the subfactors

Q_{*}(\Psi_{1}^{2}\Psi_{2}^{2}),\ Q_{*}(\Psi_{1}^{2}\Psi_{2}),\ Q_{*}(\Psi_{1}\Psi_{2}^{2}),\ Q_{*}(\Psi_{1}\Psi_{2}).

In particular,
\operatorname{Ber}\pi_{*}{\mathcal{K}}
is canonically trivial,
so

\operatorname{Ber}R\pi_{*}(\Omega_{X_{B}/B})\simeq\operatorname{Ber}R\pi_{*}\Omega_{\widetilde{X}/B}.

(iv) One has an exact sequence over the smooth locus of
B
,

0\to j_{*}\Omega_{U/\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}}|_{X_{B}}\to\Omega_{\widetilde{X}/B}(D_{1}+D_{2})\to\widetilde{{\mathcal{C}}}\to 0,

where
U\subset X
is the smooth locus of
X\to\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}
,
and the sheaf
\widetilde{{\mathcal{C}}}
is supported on the node and has a filtration with the subfactors

{\mathcal{O}}_{Q},\ {\mathcal{O}}_{Q},\ Q_{*}(\Psi_{1}^{-1}\oplus\Psi_{2}^{-1}).

Proof.

(i) We just have to identify the pull-back of the line bundle
\omega_{X_{B}/B}^{2}
to
\widetilde{X}
,
\rho^{*}\omega_{X_{B}/B}^{2}
with

\omega_{\widetilde{X}/B}^{2}(D_{1}+D_{2})
. Note that we have a natural identification of these line bundles over the smooth locus of
\widetilde{X}
.
Thus, we need to check that it extends to an isomorphism over the node. For this, it is enough to study these line bundles in an étale
neighborhood of the node. Thus, we can place ourselves in the framework of Sec.
6.2
and use a generator

e
of
\omega_{X/S}^{2}
(see (
6.13
)). Since
\rho^{*}e
is a generator of
\omega_{\widetilde{X}/B}^{2}(D_{1}+D_{2})
, the assertion follows.

(ii) First, let us consider the case
m=0
.
Using Lemma
6.4
we easily see that there is an injective map
\omega_{X_{B}/B}\to\rho_{*}\omega_{\widetilde{X}/B}(D_{1}+D_{2})
,
and that
\omega_{X_{B}/B}
contains
\rho_{*}\omega_{\widetilde{X}/B}
. Thus, we have

{\mathcal{C}}:=\operatorname{coker}(\omega_{X_{B}/B}\to\rho_{*}\omega_{\widetilde{X}/B}(D_{1}+D_{2}))=\operatorname{coker}(\omega_{X_{B}/B}/\omega_{\widetilde{X}/B}\to\rho_{*}(\omega_{\widetilde{X}/B}(D_{1}+D_{2})/\omega_{\widetilde{X}/B})).

Note that since
\Psi_{i}^{-1}\simeq P_{i}^{*}\omega_{\widetilde{X}/B}(D_{i})
,
by Remark
8.10
, we have an exact sequence

0\to{\mathcal{O}}_{P_{1}}\oplus{\mathcal{O}}_{P_{2}}\to\omega_{\widetilde{X}/B}(D_{1}+D_{2})/\omega_{\widetilde{X}/B}\to P_{1*}\Psi_{1}^{-1}\oplus P_{2*}\Psi_{2}^{-1}\to 0

In local coordinates, the projection to
P_{i*}\Psi_{i}^{-1}
sends
\frac{1}{z_{i}}[dz_{i}|d\theta_{i}]
to a generator, and sends
\frac{\theta_{i}}{z_{i}}[dz_{i}|d\theta_{i}]
to zero.
In particular, this projection vanishes on the image of
\omega_{X_{B}/B}/\omega_{\widetilde{X}/B}
.
Thus, we have an embedding

\omega_{X_{B}/B}/\omega_{\widetilde{X}/B}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{O}}_{Q}\oplus{\mathcal{O}}_{Q}.

We claim that
\omega_{X_{B}/B}/\omega_{\widetilde{X}/B}
coincides with the kernel of
the addition map
{\mathcal{O}}_{Q}\oplus{\mathcal{O}}_{Q}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{Q}
.
Indeed, this is a local statement. In local coordinates the generators of two summands
{\mathcal{O}}_{Q}
are
\frac{\theta_{i}}{z_{i}}[dz_{i}|d\theta_{i}]
,
and the generator
s_{0}
of
\omega_{X_{B}/B}/\omega_{\widetilde{X}/B}
is mapped to their difference.

From this we also see that
{\mathcal{C}}
fits into the exact sequence (
9.5
).

To derive the case of arbitrary
m
from that of
m=0
, we tensor the sequence (
9.5
) with the line bundle

\omega^{2m}_{X_{B}/B}
. By the triviality of
Q^{*}(\omega_{X_{B}/B}^{2}
, we get the sequence of the required form.

(iii) First, we note that
{\mathcal{K}}
is supported on the node, so it is enough to prove the assertion after replacing
X_{B}
with the formal neighborhood of the node.
Then
\widetilde{X}
becomes the union of two branches
X_{1}\sqcup X_{2}
, so that
P_{1}\in X_{1}
,
P_{2}\in X_{2}
.
Now we have inclusions of ideals

\rho_{*}(I_{P_{1}}\oplus 0),\rho_{*}(0\oplus I_{P_{2}})\subset{\mathcal{O}}_{X_{B}}\subset\rho_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{X}}.

Furthermore, the product of these ideals is zero. This implies that for
a_{1}\in I_{P_{1}}
,
a_{2}\in I_{P_{2}}
, one has

(0,a_{2})\cdot d(a_{1},0)=\pm(a_{1},0)\cdot d(0,a_{2}).

Hence, we have well defined map

\kappa:I_{P_{1}}/I_{P_{1}}^{2}\otimes I_{P_{2}}/I_{P_{2}}^{2}\to{\mathcal{K}}\subset\Omega_{X_{B}/B}:a_{1}\otimes a_{2})\mapsto(a_{1},0)\cdot d(0,a_{2}).

Note that
I_{P_{1}}/I_{P_{1}}^{2}
fits into an exact sequence

0\to I_{D_{1}}/I_{D_{1}}I_{P_{1}}\to I_{P_{1}}/I_{P_{1}}^{2}\to I_{P_{1}}/I_{D_{1}}\to 0

with
I_{D_{1}}/I_{D_{1}}I_{P_{1}}\simeq\Psi_{1}^{2}
and
I_{P_{1}}/I_{D_{1}}\simeq\Psi_{1}
.
Using Lemma
7.2
(ii), one checks that

\kappa
is an isomorphism, and the assertion follows.

(iv) This follows from parts (i), (ii) and from the exact sequence (
7.1
) (and a similar exact sequence for
\Omega_{\widetilde{X}/B}
which
holds over the smooth locus).
∎

We can use the definition of the NS boundary line bundle (
7.6
) to compute the normal bundle
over the smooth part of
B
.
From the above Lemma we get

\operatorname{Ber}R\pi_{*}(j_{*}\Omega_{U/S})|_{B}\simeq\operatorname{Ber}R\pi_{*}(\Omega_{\widetilde{X}/B}(D_{1}+D_{2}))\otimes\Psi_{1}^{-1}\Psi_{2}^{-1},

\operatorname{Ber}R\pi_{*}(\Omega_{X/S}|_{B})\simeq\operatorname{Ber}R\pi_{*}\Omega_{\widetilde{X}/B}.

Finally the exact sequence

0\to\omega_{\widetilde{X}/B}^{2}\to\Omega_{\widetilde{X}/B}\to\omega_{\widetilde{X}/B}\to 0

near
D_{1},D_{2}
, together with Lemma
8.7
, show that

\operatorname{Ber}R\pi_{*}\Omega_{\widetilde{X}/B}(D_{1}+D_{2})\simeq\operatorname{Ber}R\pi_{*}\Omega_{\widetilde{X}/B}.

Hence, we deduce an isomorphism

{\mathcal{O}}(\Delta)|_{B}\simeq\operatorname{Ber}R\pi_{*}(j_{*}\Omega_{U/S})|_{B}\otimes\operatorname{Ber}^{-1}R\pi_{*}(\Omega_{X/S}|_{B})\simeq\Psi_{1}^{-1}\Psi_{2}^{-1}.

(9.6)

Now we can state a compatibility result between the super Mumford isomorphisms over
\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}
and over the NS boundary component
B
.
Recall that we have the super Mumford isomorphism

\mu_{\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}}:{\mathcal{B}}(\omega_{X/\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}}^{-2})\rTo{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})^{5}(-\Delta)

near
B
(see Prop.
8.5
). We also have the super Mumford isomorphism for the family
\widetilde{X}/B
, over the smooth locus:

\mu_{B}:{\mathcal{B}}(\omega_{\widetilde{X}/B}^{-2}(-D_{1}-D_{2}))\rTo{\sim}{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{O}}_{\widetilde{X}})^{5}\otimes\Psi_{1}\otimes\Psi_{2}

(see Lemma
8.11
).

Theorem 9.2
.

The following diagram of isomorphisms of line bundles on
B
is commutative up to a sign

{diagram}

Recall that to get
\mu_{\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}}
we used three isomorphisms: the Grothendieck-Serre duality isomoprhism

SD_{X/\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}}:{\mathcal{B}}(\omega_{X/\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}}^{-2})\rTo{\sim}{\mathcal{B}}(\omega_{X/\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}}^{3}),

the isomorphism

{\mathcal{B}}(\omega_{X/\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}}^{3})\simeq{\mathcal{B}}(\omega_{X/\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}}^{2})^{-1}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})^{2},

and the isomorphism

{\mathcal{B}}(\omega_{X/\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}}^{2})\simeq{\mathcal{B}}(\omega_{X/\overline{{\mathcal{S}}}})^{-2}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{O}}_{X})^{-1}(\Delta)

(see Sec.
8.3
). Similarly,
\mu_{B}
is a composition of similar three isomorphisms. So we can reduce the proof of Theorem
9.2

to separate compatibilities involving each of these three isomorphisms. We deal with this compatibilities in the next three lemmas.

Lemma 9.3
.

For any
m\in\Z
, the following diagram of isomorphisms between line bundles on
